20. **The declared-outcome legality gate goes stale for the entire duration of a resumed run.** After `run resume` against a declared `BLOCKED`, the run row still reads `BLOCKED` while a live scheduler relaunches attempts — nothing may reset it, since NULL is defined as "never declared" — so the escape verbs and a second `run resume` are admitted against a live scheduler, which is the exact race §7.3's own rationale says the gate exists to prevent. Severity as assessed by R7: P1. Concrete failure: `abandon` writes absolutely-terminal `CANCELLED` under a relaunched worker's feet, whose later `RUNNING→VERIFIED` transition against a terminal state is specified nowhere; a second `run resume` starts a second live scheduler outright. Discharge: a legality fact that actually tracks scheduler liveness — a run-level lease or heartbeat on the run row, allowlisted with a stated reason, with escapes and resume refused while it is fresh — or a specified and tested transactional contract for each escape verb against a live scheduler; §17 item 105's negatives extended to the resumed-live case.
21. **Resume against a live, never-declared scheduler produces two concurrent schedulers with no mutual exclusion.** NULL is indistinguishable from "crashed", `run resume` is legal against NULL, and §7.3's non-adoption sentence defends only against adopting live work — nothing prevents two live schedulers holding two merge threads over one integration branch and two watchdogs over each other's attempts. Severity as assessed by R7: P1. Concrete failure: an operator wrongly asserts a slow scheduler is dead and resumes; two `merge --no-ff` sequences interleave against one integration branch, violating §8.5's determinism at best and corrupting the run at worst. Discharge: the same lease as item 20 — resume takes an exclusive run-level lease the running scheduler maintains, so the wrong-assertion case degrades to a refusal — with a negative test that resume against a live lease-holding scheduler refuses.
22. **§8.3 and §8.4 classify one event class two ways.** §8.3 says a dirty tree at the first cleanliness evaluation — post-commit, pre-gate — "convicts", while §8.4 classifies a write in the same nothing-may-write window as ENVIRONMENTAL, and §8.3's own attribution paragraph says a write after the after-inventory is never the node's. Severity as assessed by R7's consistency pass: P1. Concrete failure: an implementer following §8.3's cleanliness sentence builds a non-retryable node conviction where the owning classification rule demands a retryable ENVIRONMENTAL failure, for the same machine fact. Discharge: one owning classification for writes in the post-measurement harness-only window, stated identically in §8.3 and §8.4, with a negative test distinguishing the two cleanliness evaluations' consequences.
23. **The document's pass count and register count went stale with every round.** The front matter asserted a four-pass history, a four-term severity curve, "the latest fixes have not themselves been attacked", and "a fifth pass" long after §17's own record showed R5 through R9 had run; item 12 repeated all four claims; and an R8 half-update left the front matter asserting the four-pass and the eight-pass world within six lines. Severity as assessed by R7's consistency pass: P1 — a reader trusting the header's convergence claim would treat attacked-and-convicted fixes as unattacked. Discharged by R10: the front matter and item 12 are recounted directly from §17's dated additions and this register's numbering, and the rule is stated where the counts live — they are derived from that record, never restated by hand. Recorded here because the failure pattern — a narrative summary maintained by hand beside a dated record that contradicts it — is the RC1 shape applied to the document about itself, and it recurred across four rounds before being closed.
24. **A declared-output symlink with an out-of-worktree target lets the gates validate content the commit does not carry.** The tuple records the link text, both conjuncts pass for a declared absent-at-baseline path, but the gates execute in the worktree and dereference the link, validating an implementation that lives in a temp directory the branch will never contain. Severity as assessed by R7: P2. Concrete failure: an `ACCEPTED` run whose integration branch holds a symlink to `/tmp/build/calc.py` — the deliverable dangles everywhere except the machine that built it. Discharge: at the permission check, refuse or convict any delta symlink whose target resolves outside the attempt's worktree — a per-path resolution check on measured state, not a name-shaped permitted set — or accept the residual here by name with a documenting negative test.
25. **The tuple's mode-class vocabulary omits gitlinks, so git's committable resolution is four classes where the inventory has three.** A nested repository or submodule produces a gitlink (mode 160000) the inventory cannot represent; behaviour is undefined — crash, misclassify a nested tree as thousands of regular files, or skip — and a glob-covered nested repo would commit a gitlink pointing at an unfetchable commit. Severity as assessed by R7: P2. Concrete failure: an agent clones a reference repo inside its worktree under a declared glob and the integration branch carries a permanently dangling submodule pointer through a green run. Discharge: add the gitlink class with defined semantics — simplest, any gitlink or nested-repository entry in the delta convicts unconditionally for both node kinds, since the merge protocol cannot carry submodule content — with a negative test.
26. **Shared-cache poisoning has no in-run detection point; item 14's "final integration gate" backstop claim is wrong.** The final gate's fresh tree is provisioned from the same shared cache the poison sits in, so where the ecosystem trusts cache content the gate re-derives the poisoned dependency and validates against it — the claimed backstop reproduces the poison. Severity as assessed by R7: P2. Concrete failure: an item-15-class absolute-path write into the provision cache passes every gate including final acceptance. Discharge: replace item 14's sentence "cache poisoning there — like any absolute-path write, item 15 — is invisible until the final integration gate re-runs in a fresh tree" with: "cache poisoning there — like any absolute-path write, item 15 — has no in-run detection point at all: the final gate's fresh tree provisions from the same cache, so the bound is the ecosystem's own integrity checking (lockfile hashes) plus item 15's general residual, and nothing in Maestro detects it."
27. **§17 item 93 enumerates two of §5.3's three added allowlist facts.** Item 93 predates the `latest run outcome` addition and was never amended, so a checklist-driven implementation of the guard allowlist omits the entry item 105 requires `run resume` to read. Severity as assessed by R7's consistency pass: P2. Concrete failure: an allowlist built from item 93 makes the three-valued resume legality check of item 105 unimplementable without an off-list read. Discharge: amend item 93 to read: "§5.3's guard allowlist extended with `granted extra attempts`, `last transition timestamp`, and `latest run outcome`, each with its stated reason." Discharged by R12: item 93 now enumerates all four allowlist additions — `granted extra attempts`, `last transition timestamp`, `latest run outcome`, and the R12-added `recorded liveness bounds`. Recorded here so the stale-enumeration pattern stays on the register.
28. **The two shared-core responsibility enumerations omit the process-group duty.** §9.6 and §12.2 step 7 both present themselves as the complete statement of what the shared core does and neither mentioned process groups or quiesce. Severity as assessed by R7's consistency pass: P2. Concrete failure: an implementer building the shared core from either list ships it without the group machinery §8.3 and §14.3 price into it. Discharge: this entry is discharged in full by R8's rewrite of both lists; it is recorded here only so that the omission remains on the register rather than vanishing.
29. **§2.10 still recommends the constructor-parameter mechanism the design rejects.** The baseline seam analysis calls an optional `repo_root` constructor argument on `Run` "the minimal change", while §12.2 step 1.6 and §17 item 7 reject exactly that mechanism because one `Run` is shared by all concurrent nodes. Severity as assessed by R7's consistency pass: P2. Concrete failure: an implementer reading the seam analysis first threads the working directory through the shared constructor and every concurrent node resolves the same worktree. Discharge: one sentence in §2.10 noting the recommendation is superseded for the concurrent case and pointing to §12.2 step 1.6.
30. **§9.8's receipt inventory does not register the group-membership receipt that four sections send the reader to it for.** §8.3 conditions the agent-node group-kill upgrade on "an executed §9.8 receipt proving the group's membership excludes the pane shell and every sibling attempt", item 17 names the same receipt as its discharge condition, §17 item 101 requires it, and §6.5's finalization window points its herdr-spawned reviewer's kill gap at the same receipt (the fourth citation, added by R12; count corrected by R14) — but §9.8's executed-claims table and its statement of what remains unrun ("Still unrun for omp: merging a verified node, ancestry proof, and post-merge acceptance. Still unrun entirely for Claude: all of it.") list no such receipt, executed or pending. Severity as assessed by R9's consistency pass: P2. Concrete consequence: the receipt has four citations and no owner. A reader following any of them reaches §9.8 and finds nothing to execute, and an implementer working §9.8's remaining rows — which §12.2 step 8 treats as required implementation steps, not a formality — ships without ever attempting it, so item 17 stays open by omission rather than by decision and the agent-node quiesce upgrade has no scheduled evidence. This is registered rather than repaired deliberately: what the receipt must show, and whether its home is §9.8's probe inventory or a herdr-capability section that does not exist yet, is a design decision the round that found this was not authorized to take. Discharge: add the group-membership receipt to §9.8's pending inventory with its acceptance criteria stated there — the launch path under test, the enumeration proving the group excludes the pane shell and every sibling attempt, and a planted survivor the group kill must terminate — or, if it belongs elsewhere, name that home in §9.8 so the four citing sections resolve to something. **Discharged 2026-08-19.** §9.8 now registers the receipt with those five acceptance criteria, its home decided as §9.8 on the grounds that what it establishes is a property of one launch path under a named herdr version, which is what every other row there records. The four citations now resolve. Discharging this says the receipt has an owner and criteria; it does not say the receipt passed — §9.8 records it as partially executed, with sibling exclusion and the planted-survivor kill still unrun, so item 17 below stays open.
31. **The silence rule is a design invariant with no mechanical detector; its enforcement is review.** §11.2's quantified prohibition — every plan-content-duration stretch of harness-launched execution runs inside a bounded window — is asserted by prose and tested only at its named instances: §17 items 64, 66, 106, 107, 108, and 110 pin the three windows and their refresh, arming, and refusal points, and no test, detector, or ledger row asserts the general property over execution not yet written. A contributor adding a new plan-content-duration step outside a window — §8.8 names per-merge verification as exactly such future work — ships with every existing test green. The reviewer heuristic in §13.5 names the question; this entry records that a heuristic is all there is, per the document's own rule that claiming automated enforcement that does not exist is a defect. Discharge, if ever: a mechanical detector over the implementation (for example, a ledger row binding each window's timeout to a kill-and-record site), which no round has designed and this round does not pretend to.
32. **The gate-executability collection executes the runner's collector at plan time, outside every bounded window — the B14 hang shape relocated into validation, and no plan-time backstop exists behind it.** §6.4's scoped obligation requires, for every gate over paths that exist at base, that "the collection count is checked as stated" — checkable only by executing the runner's collector (`pytest --collect-only`, the vitest equivalent) against a real tree, which is plan-content-duration execution by §11.2's own enumeration, run by `plan validate`, again by `plan finalize`'s re-validation, and again at run start (§5.2: `validate.py` is called once, by authoring, finalization, and run-start alike). Concrete failure: a selector whose collection imports a module that blocks on the network at import time wedges the collector, and the verb waits forever — no timeout, no liveness signal, no typed outcome, no diagnostic. §11.2 previously classified validation's obligations wholesale as seconds-scale bookkeeping; that sentence is now narrowed to name this exception rather than hide it. A secondary gap compounds it: for a JS runner, collection requires a provisioned tree, and nothing states whether validation provisions (more unbounded plan-time execution) or collects against an unprovisioned tree (a deterministic false blocker). Severity as assessed by R13: P1. Consequence for the silence rule's generality: the closure is established over run execution and over reviewer execution inside the finalization window, and **not** over plan-time validation — no section may claim it fully general while this item is open, and §11.2 now says so in its own text. Discharge: either demote the collection-count obligation out of plan time — well-formedness at validation, per §6.4's own produced-path precedent, with §7.4's run-time pre-gate doing the real falsification inside the attempt window, which needs no new window — or declare obligation execution a fourth bounded window carrying all three of §11.2's obligations, with the provisioning question answered explicitly; in either case a negative test in which a planted never-returning collector must produce a typed, bounded outcome at every one of the three call sites, never a silent hang.
33. **The finalization window opens at reviewer launch, so the launch path itself — pane allocation, `herdr agent start`, the tracer's reviewer-session row write — runs before the window, unbounded, with no plan-time backstop behind it.** A herdr server that accepts the connection but never completes pane allocation (busy, wedged, or the pane budget consumed by a concurrent run) leaves `plan finalize` blocked inside the adapter's launch call: the reviewer-session row is written "at launch", which has not happened; the window has not opened; the finalization wall-clock timeout bounds a span that does not exist; §7.6's signals arm only at reported launch. The verb waits indefinitely, printing nothing — B14's silent indefinite wait reproduced one step earlier than R12's repair reaches. R12's own run-side fix closed exactly this shape for attempt windows by opening the window before provision and launch (§7.6's pre-launch segment); the finalization window received no analogous coverage, and §17 item 108's negative tests concede the gap — each of them presupposes a reviewer that already launched, and none exercises a launch call that never returns. Severity as assessed by R13: P1. Consequence for the silence rule's generality: the closure is established over reviewer execution inside the window and **not** over the launch path that precedes it — stated in §11.2 alongside item 32's gap. Discharge: open the finalization window when the verb begins the launch, with the durable record generalized honestly — a verb-written launch-initiation row in the tracer before the adapter is called, the same store, one write earlier — the finalization timeout covering launch plus review as one span, §7.6's signals still arming at reported launch, and a launch that never reports converted at the same timeout into the same kill-where-possible and `FINALIZATION_STALLED` with the route recorded and the session id recorded as absent; §17 item 108 gains the missing negative: a launch call that never returns must be declared `FINALIZATION_STALLED` at the finalization timeout, not waited on. *(Superseded 2026-08-20: plan finalization dispatches no reviewer, §6.5. Retained as the record of what was unproven while it did; the finalization window and its liveness bounds survive only on the node-review path.)*
34. **The turn timeout is a fourth liveness value, read live by the watchdog, pinned nowhere — so §9.5's sentence "a configuration edit mid-run changes nothing a live scheduler consults" is false as stated.** Preflight pins exactly three values (`T`, the node wall-clock timeout, the final-acceptance timeout) into the run row; the turn timeout is read at runtime by the same watchdog (§7.6), appears in §6.5 and §17 item 108, and is in no pinned set, no §5.3 allowlist entry, and no §10.3 column. Concrete failure: an operator editing timeout configuration mid-run fat-fingers the turn timeout; the watchdog's next poll reads the live value; every healthy agent mid-turn is declared stalled, killed, and retried ENVIRONMENTAL until nodes block — a run-wide misfire reached without failing any preflight, which is word-for-word the hazard §5.3's `recorded liveness bounds` reason gives as the motivation for pinning. The finalization verb's two bounds (finalization timeout, turn timeout) are likewise unpinned, with no stated read-once rule, though a single-process verb makes pinning trivial. Severity as assessed by R13: P2. Discharge: add the turn timeout to the preflight-recorded set — one more value in the same run-row record, re-recorded at resume, needing no `T` inequality, only pinning — or narrow §9.5's universal sentence to the three recorded values by name and register the turn timeout as deliberately live-read with this misfire named beside it; plus one sentence in §6.5 stating that the finalize verb reads its two bounds once at invocation.
35. **Rerun-after-stall reviewer identity is unspecified: nothing keys a rerun's session directory away from the stalled predecessor's, and on the herdr path the predecessor survives unkilled.** §6.5 requires the review to run in "a fresh session directory, never the authoring node's" — a contrast with authoring, not with a prior review attempt — and finalization has no attempt number, so the design's session-keying precedent (§9.6's unique directory per node attempt; §10.4's base defect of two live agents in one session) has no finalization analogue. Concrete failure: a slow honest review on the herdr route exceeds the finalization timeout; the verb cannot kill (item 17's shared gap), declares `FINALIZATION_STALLED`, and exits; the operator reruns `plan finalize` on the same digest; an implementation keying the reviewer session on the digest re-enters the surviving reviewer's directory — two live agents in one session directory, an appending shared output file, and the predecessor's late report or contaminated context feeding the second verb's canary-passing review, which is §10.4's fixed defect shape reintroduced at plan time. Concurrent `plan finalize` invocations on one digest are likewise unserialized — the create-once receipt adjudicates the end of the race, not the overlap. Severity as assessed by R13: P2. Discharge: one sentence in §6.5 keying the reviewer session per finalization invocation — digest plus a per-invocation nonce, mirroring the per-attempt rule — stating that a rerun after `FINALIZATION_STALLED` never reuses or continues the stalled session and that the surviving pane's late output lands in a directory no subsequent verb reads; §17 item 108 gains the corresponding negative. *(Superseded 2026-08-20: plan finalization dispatches no reviewer, §6.5. Retained as the record of what was unproven while it did; the finalization window and its liveness bounds survive only on the node-review path.)*
36. **The post-commit cleanliness check convicted an attempt over baseline-present untracked content that provision or the pre-gate created, contradicting §13.3's own required negative control.** §8.3's baseline inventory deliberately includes untracked paths — "no excludes, no ignore list" — and that inclusion is load-bearing: it is what lets conjunct (2) convict tampering with provisioned untracked content. The commit set, however, is only the **measured delta**, so untracked content that was present at baseline and that the agent never touched is correctly absent from the commit. §8.4's `git read-tree <output_sha>` refresh then restores the worktree's index to the committed tree, which reconciles the harness's own staging debt over tracked content and nothing else: baseline-present untracked paths remain untracked afterwards. §8.3's first cleanliness evaluation, as originally worded, compared against HEAD and the index and supplied no comparison against the provisioned baseline at all, so it read those paths as stray writes. The document's own §13.3 negative control states the required behaviour verbatim: "**a file created by the pre-gate does not convict while an identically-named file created by the agent does**" (§8.3); as originally worded, the first half of that control could not pass. This is adjacent to item 19 but distinct and not covered by it: item 19 is provision **mutating a tracked path**, where the divergence is against HEAD; this is baseline-present **untracked** content that the commit is right to omit. Severity as assessed by R15: P1. Concrete failure, as originally worded: a pre-gate that leaves any untracked byproduct the scratch redirection does not capture — a coverage artifact, a report file, an adapter's own residue — made every attempt of every node on that runner fail the post-commit cleanliness evaluation, convicting an agent that never touched the file, which is the infra-flavoured misclassification shape `CODE_NODE_NO_EFFECT` exists to avoid. This was empirically confirmed by an executed composition probe on 2026-08-13: both fully-verified nodes convicted at this evaluation solely because of `.pre_gate_cache`, a pre-gate byproduct the baseline deliberately included, while the same check evaluated against the expected full inventory acquitted both. A separate operator ruling excluding dot-prefixed paths from inventory measurement does not reach this defect, because the check as originally worded consulted `git status` directly, never the inventory. **Discharged:** §8.3 now defines both cleanliness evaluations over the **expected full inventory** — the baseline inventory unioned with the measured, committed delta, which is exactly the after-inventory taken at settle — rather than against HEAD or `git status`; a path matching the expected inventory convicts no one for merely still being present, and any divergence from it still convicts (post-commit) or is reported (pre-merge, unchanged). §8.4's parallel description of what the index refresh leaves comparable is corrected to match. This is a **state comparison** against content harness-controlled execution created and measured before any untrusted code ran — it is not an ignore list and not a name-shaped permitted set, both of which §8.3 rejects on principle and which remain rejected: no path is permitted by name, and a renamed or relocated version of the same byproduct is judged on its own measured tuple, never on its name. Negative test: §13.3's control above, executed at the post-commit evaluation — the identically-named agent-created file still convicts, and the pre-gate-created file no longer does. Provenance: found by R15, an independent-model-family read-only verification pass; confirmed and discharged following the executed composition probe of 2026-08-13.
37. **R15's coverage was not demonstrated, so its single finding is not evidence that the rest of the document is clean.** The pass ran read-only against the frozen document and returned a 1,364-byte report produced in roughly 200 seconds over a 425 KB, seventeen-section text. It shows no evidence of the internal-consistency sweep it was asked to perform, no programmatic verification of §17's or this register's numbering, and no account of which attacks were attempted and returned nothing. A report that does not state its coverage cannot distinguish a section that was examined and found sound from a section that was not examined, so R15's one P1 (item 36) bounds nothing beyond itself: it establishes that a defect exists in §8.3, not that defects are absent elsewhere. That the finding it did return had survived eight prior Claude-family passes is itself evidence that unexamined surface is likely to hold more. This document has never achieved a fully clean round; the stability rule requires two *consecutive clean* passes, and the count of clean passes is zero (item 12). Registered rather than argued away, per the standing rule that what remains unproven is stated exactly and consensus is never fabricated. Discharge: an independent pass whose report states its own coverage — the sections read, the attacks attempted with their outcomes including the null ones, and the numbering checks performed programmatically — so that silence on a section is a measurement rather than an absence of one.
38. **`Run.save_agent_map`'s in-process fix does not close the cross-process form of the same race.** Step 1's execution (§12.2 step 1.9, §10.4) found and fixed a race where the map save serialised a stale in-memory copy loaded at `Run` construction, dropping a concurrent sibling's entry; the fix — a re-read of `agent_map.json` under a lock immediately before merge, then an atomic replace — closes that race for every thread sharing one `Run`, which is Maestro's actual concurrency shape for nodes within a single scheduler process (§12.2 step 1.6, §7.2). What it does not reach is two separate ADW **processes** writing the same run's map: an in-process lock guards nothing across a process boundary, so two processes each performing their own lock-guarded read-merge-write can still interleave their reads before either writes, reproducing the identical drop the Step 1 fix closed for threads. Severity: unassessed by any adversarial round — found directly by Step 1's execution, not by review. Concrete failure: the scenario this design already names as reachable, §16.3 item 21 and item 20's resume-without-mutual-exclusion gap — an operator wrongly resuming a run whose scheduler is still alive, producing two live schedulers each with their own `Run` — would have both processes writing `agent_map.json` for the same `adw_id`, and the in-process lock in either process does nothing to serialise them against each other. Discharge: file locking over `agent_map.json` itself (e.g. an advisory lock held for the duration of the read-merge-write, honoured by every writer regardless of process), or closing the underlying two-live-schedulers hazard first (items 20–21's lease), which would remove the only path Slice 1 has to two processes holding one run's map open at once; a negative test planting two processes racing a save against one map must show neither entry dropped.
39. **Node-gate scope is a rule the eligibility obligations can only partly decide.** §6.2 and §7.4 require an agent node's gate to be scoped to that node's own work, and §6.4's twelfth obligation refuses the two forms of the violation that are decidable from plan bytes: a gate whose command core equals the plan's integration gate's, and an argv naming no selector at all. Every other over-selection is statically indistinguishable from an honest gate — a directory selector that happens to reach a sibling's tests, a marker expression, a base-existing test that only passes once a sibling merges — because whether a selector reaches beyond a node's work is a fact about behaviour at a base, not about the argv. Concrete failure: an author scopes a gate one directory too wide; the pre-node gate is red as required, the post-node gate is red as well but for the sibling's absent work, the failure classifies SEMANTIC because a failed gate is exactly that class's trigger (§7.5), and the node is re-prompted against every advancing base until `SEMANTIC_BUDGET_EXHAUSTED` blocks a node whose own work was correct throughout — an authoring defect reported as a content failure. Severity as assessed at registration: P2, because it blocks one node with a diagnosable gate result rather than deadlocking the run, which is what the scope rule was introduced to prevent. Why it is not discharged here: both known discharges are refused elsewhere in this document. Deciding it at plan time means running each node's collector against a tree and comparing collected cases against the sibling set, which is a plan-content-duration execution inside validation — the shape item 33 already has open and §11.2 forbids outside a bounded window. Deciding it at run time means attributing a red gate to an absent sibling, which requires reading *which* cases failed and mapping them to another node's outputs — a per-case attribution the §10.2 counting rule deliberately does not expose, and one that would reintroduce ecosystem-specific report parsing beyond the five counts every runner reduces to. Registered rather than argued away. Provenance: the two-node composition probe executed on 2026-08-13, the same probe that convicted the whole-suite reading of the post-node gate (§7.4).
40. **§10.2's counting rule parsed five counts and consulted four, so a gate with real failures passed.** The rule was stated as `passed >= min_cases >= 1 AND skipped < collected AND errored == 0` over a parser producing `(collected, passed, failed, skipped, errored)`. `failed` appeared in the tuple and in no clause. Concrete failure: an agent node whose own scoped gate reports one pass and one failure satisfies the conjunction at `min_cases = 1` — one passing case is enough, nothing skipped, nothing errored — so §7.3 clause 3 holds, the node reaches `VERIFIED`, and it merges with the behaviour its gate exists to witness demonstrably absent. This is not a narrow edge: `min_cases` is a floor on passing cases, not a claim about the rest of the collection, so any node whose selector reaches more cases than `min_cases` is exposed, which is most of them. It also silently defeats §7.4 — the pre-gate must FAIL and the post-gate must PASS, and the only thing separating those two runs is the agent's work, so admitting a red post-gate as a pass makes the differential prove nothing. Severity as assessed at registration: P0 for correctness of the acceptance predicate; the design's central claim is that nothing merges unverified, and this admitted unverified work by arithmetic. **Discharged:** §10.2 now states the conjunction with `failed == 0` and explains the omission; §7.3 clause 3's restatement is corrected to match; and the rule is implemented in `adw_modules/verification.py` with a test that a report of one pass and one failure is not green. Provenance: found by implementing §10.2 and running it, 2026-08-13 — not by any adversarial round. Four prior rounds read this conjunction and none noticed that one of the five counts it parses was never consulted, which is the same lesson as items 36 and 39: the defects that survive review are the ones whose text reads fluently.
41. **A deterministic test failed twice and the cause is attributed rather than reproduced.** During the Step 2 and Step 3 lanes, `test_finalization_window.SignalsArmAtReportedLaunch.test_the_turn_clock_starts_at_launch_not_at_open` failed once with `AssertionError: unexpectedly None`, and a second lane recorded one unexplained `errors=1` in the same period. Both occurred while other lanes were writing files into the tree; four subsequent runs on a quiet tree were clean, and the suite has since passed on every run. The test is deterministic by construction — it drives a `FakeClock`, injects every collaborator as a lambda, and touches no real clock, no subprocess, no temporary directory, and no thread — so it has no per-run input that could vary, which leaves only two candidates: state shared with another test in the same process, or the interpreter executing bytes other than the ones on disk. The second is a measured property of this environment rather than a hypothesis: `sys.pycache_prefix` caches compiled modules outside the repository, cache entries are validated on source mtime and size, and an edit preserving size within the same second is therefore invisible — a race whose window is exactly "a lane is writing files while the suite runs". Severity as assessed at registration: P2 — it corrupts the evidence the suite exists to produce, in both directions, but it is a harness property and reaches no shipped behaviour. **Partially discharged:** `adw_test.py` now redirects `sys.pycache_prefix` back to the source tree, removes the caches there, and sets `sys.dont_write_bytecode` before the loader imports anything, so no run after this one can read or write a stale entry. What is **not** discharged is the attribution itself: the original failure was never reproduced, so the stale-bytecode explanation is inference from elimination, not measurement, and the shared-module-state candidate has not been ruled out by execution. Discharge: either a reproduction of the original failure against a deliberately staled cache, or a recurrence on a quiet tree after this fix, which would convict the remaining candidate instead. Provenance: observed by the Step 2 and Step 3 lanes, 2026-08-13; registered rather than closed, because a flake that stopped appearing is not a flake that was explained.

42. **`NodeExecution.launcher_failure` has a reader and no writer, so LAUNCHER_TRANSIENT is unreachable from the real adapter.** This is B15's lesson inverted: B15 convicts a check field with zero readers, and the same defect exists in the other direction, where the reader is present, the classifier branch behind it is present, and nothing ever supplies the value. `scheduler.py::NodeExecution.launcher_failure` (L82) is read at `scheduler.py::Scheduler._settle_verdict` (L834) into the `FailureSignal`, and `retry_policy.py::classify` (L172) branches on it to return `RetryClass.LAUNCHER_TRANSIENT`. A scan of the template for `launcher_failure` finds five other occurrences and **no assignment on any production path**: the field's definition, the `FailureSignal` field it feeds, and two tests that construct `rp.FailureSignal` directly (`tests/test_retry_policy.py:121`, `:390`), bypassing `NodeExecution` entirely. The real adapter's `run_node` never sets it. Consequences, both current: every launcher failure falls through to the ENVIRONMENTAL default and spends the wrong retry budget, and §7.5's zero-budget rule for `LauncherFailure.CREDENTIAL` — the one launcher class that must never be retried — cannot fire at all. The two tests are green over a path production cannot reach, which is G5's lesson in a second instance. The adjacent half of the same gap: the launcher's typed poll vocabulary (`AGENT_GONE`, `NO_ENVELOPE`, `ENVELOPE_UNPARSED`, `LAUNCH_REFUSED:*`) lives in `PollResult.detail` and is discarded inside `run_node` on every branch it has, so it reaches neither the classifier nor the ledger; §7.6's detail fix records the watchdog's answer and cannot record the launcher's. Severity: P1 — a live misclassification affecting retry budgets, not merely a missing diagnostic. **Discharged**, 2026-08-18, and the ruling this item held open is recorded rather than taken silently. The writer is `maestro.py::_typed_launch`, which wraps every launcher call in the module and converts a failure into `scheduler.LaunchFailed` — a typed exception carrying an `rp.LauncherFailure` member derived by `_launcher_failure_for` from `LauncherAdapter.classify`, the adapter-contract method that had been part of the seam since it was written and had never had a production caller. `_typed_launch_pane` is the same path for the three pane-opening sites, and no raw `.launch(` call site remains in `maestro.py`, so the structural guard in `tests/test_launcher_classification.py` needs no allowlist. `Scheduler._run_node`'s containment handler reads `exc.failure` into the `FailureSignal`, which is the first input that makes `classify`'s launcher branch reachable from the real adapter; every other exception still falls to the ENVIRONMENTAL default, unchanged and fail-closed. The zero-budget rule now fires: `Classification` carries the member forward, `_settle_failure` sizes the budget with `rp.launcher_retry_budget` rather than `config.retry_budget` — because §7.5 gives CREDENTIAL zero and the rest one or two, which one number per class cannot express — and `_budget_reason` blocks a credential refusal as `CREDENTIAL_REFUSED`, a `BlockReason` §11.3's escape table already carried a row for and nothing could produce. **The ruling on `PollResult.detail`: it becomes a field on `NodeExecution`, and it is split by what may read it.** `launcher_failure` is typed and drives classification, derived from the exception's *type*, which §7.5 names among the facts a classifier may read. `launch_detail` is prose, reaches `Classification.reason` and `transitions.detail_json`, and no guard reads it back — §7.6 already established that a typed reason computed at the point of failure and dropped before the ledger is indistinguishable, to every later reader, from no reason. Branching a retry class on `launch_detail` would be the lexical shortcut §7.5 forbids, and the launcher settled the identical question for itself long ago (`HerdrCallError` branches on `.code`, never on the message). Item 43's half of the shared decision is **not** discharged by this: widening `NodeExecution` by two fields the scheduler may learn from an execution is a narrower ruling than giving the scheduler a result payload to record, and the `results` table still has no production writer. Provenance: found 2026-08-17 while tracing why `run-14b7b75944094c52ac9c0add41ae46a2` recorded empty failure details; discharged 2026-08-18.

43. **The `results` table has no production writer, so §7.7's late-arrival mechanism is inert and `run status` renders a section that can never be non-empty.** The read path is live and the write path is tests only. Sole writer: `lifecycle.py::LifecycleStore.record_result` (L691), whose only call sites in the whole template are `tests/test_step4_ledger.py:189`, `:203`, and `:222`. Its adjudicator `verification.py::adjudicate_result` (L456) is the same shape — seven call sites, every one in `tests/test_verification.py`. Nothing on any production path constructs a `ResultRecord` or records one. Meanwhile `lifecycle.py::LifecycleReader.results` (L1368) is read by `maestro.py::_run_progress` (L2158, at L2165) and rendered by `maestro.py::_render_progress` (L2249, at L2313) as `result <node_id>#<attempt_no> <adjudication>` followed by the payload JSON — an operator-visible section of `run status` that no run can ever populate. Measured read-only against the live deployment ledger on 2026-08-17: 10 runs, 25 attempts, **0 rows in `results`**. Two sections depend on the table: §7.7 specifies it in full ("Every result lands in one row", carrying payload and adjudication together, retained across all four outcomes, and "where a reclaimed attempt's late result lands … the direct repair of a correct FAIL carrying two real findings that vanished with a byte-identical journal"), and §7.9 counts its writes ("The results count scales with workload, not graph size"). Both describe a mechanism that has never executed. Why it is unfixed: the runner adapter returns `scheduler.py::NodeExecution`, which carries only `envelope_parsed`, `exit_code`, `launched_pid`, and `launcher_failure`, and whose docstring makes the narrowness deliberate — the scheduler never possesses a result payload to record. Closing this requires widening the runner adapter protocol or adding a separate result-ingest path, which is the **same decision** as item 42: both are consequences of `NodeExecution` being deliberately structural, and one ruling on what the scheduler may know discharges both. Severity: P2 as assessed here — nothing currently misbehaves, because §7.8 makes reclaim cleanup-only and no path is known to produce a late result at all. That last point is itself unestablished and is part of what discharge must settle: if any path *can* produce one, a correct result has nowhere to land, which is §7.7's originating incident restored rather than repaired. Discharge: either a production writer with a test that drives it through the real adapter, or a decision — recorded here — that the table and its `run status` section are removed until the mechanism exists, since a rendered section that cannot be populated is a standing false suggestion to an operator that the system is watching for something. Provenance: carried from a prior session's working notes as an unrecorded claim, re-verified line by line before registration on 2026-08-17; three of its line numbers had moved and are corrected above. **Discharged, verified 2026-08-18:** the first discharge arm landed. `scheduler.py::Scheduler._record_result` (L1131) is called from the settle path (L991), adjudicates through `verification.adjudicate_result` (L1160), and writes through `store.record_result` (L1164); `tests/test_result_ingest.py` drives it. **A residual is recorded rather than closed with it, and it is a defect in the register's own machinery.** The three `test_no_dead_seams.py` allowlist entries that made this item findable — `lifecycle.record_result`, `lifecycle.audit_results`, `verification.adjudicate_result`, each carrying "DEFERRED D7: … no production writer" — were still present and still asserting that after the writer landed. That file's mechanism converts "nobody calls this" into a named work item, and it is one-directional: it fails when a test-only symbol is *absent* from the allowlist, and never when an allowlisted symbol *acquires* a caller. So an entry outlives the defect it names, and this register item, which was written from those entries, went stale with them. Registered as item 51.

44. **A plan's `min_cases` never reached the adjudicator, so every gate in every run was counted against a threshold of 1.** `plan_model.Plan.to_plan_nodes()` projects the authored plan onto `scheduler_types.PlanNode`; for an agent node it copied the gate's runner, its argv, and its selector, and dropped the gate's `min_cases`, because `PlanNode` had no field to hold one. All three adjudication sites — the pre-node gate, the post-node gate, and final acceptance — read `SchedulerDeps.min_cases` instead, a single per-run scalar with no production writer, so all three counted against its default. That scalar could not have been right in any case: a run has many node gates, each declaring its own threshold, and one number per run structurally cannot carry them. Concrete failure: a plan declaring `min_cases: 70` renders 70 into the agent's prompt, the agent is verified against one passing case, and the node merges with 69 of the cases its gate exists to witness never counted; `run-4ee9e079f9124b8cbe4c416923e34170` reached ACCEPTED that way on 2026-08-17. This is item 40's defect one hop upstream — 40 was a counting rule that consulted four of the five counts it parsed, this is the same rule adjudicating against a threshold the plan never supplied — and it is why the two share a lesson: the arithmetic in §10.2 is only as strong as the inputs that reach it, and nothing was checking that they did. Severity as assessed at registration: P0 for correctness of the acceptance predicate, on the same ground as item 40. **Discharged:** `PlanNode.gate_min_cases` carries the per-gate value, validated in `__post_init__` — at least 1 on an agent node, per §10.2's floor, and exactly the default on a code node, which has no gate to count cases for and for which a threshold would be a number nothing reads; `SchedulerDeps.min_cases` is deleted and `SchedulerDeps.integration_min_cases` replaces it for the one integration gate per run that the old field was accidentally right about; and the three sites read the node's value or the run's, whichever they are adjudicating. `tests/test_min_cases_enforcement.py` drives a declared threshold through projection to each adjudication site. Provenance: found 2026-08-17 by the reader-without-writer sweep that also produced `tests/test_no_dead_seams.py`, which is the mechanism that saw it — a grep for `min_cases` cannot, because the plan gate's own same-named keyword argument masks the scheduler field's absence of writers at every call site. **Recurrence, 2026-08-18:** the discharge above was a one-field fix, and the same projection was then found to be dropping `instruction` as well, with the reviewer contract of §3.6 B9 degenerate in production as a result (§19 M1). The lesson item 44 should have carried was not "carry `min_cases`" but "this projection is total or it is a latent defect"; it is now carried by a class-level totality guard and by §17 item 115, and the per-field shape of this discharge is exactly why there was a second field left to find. **Second recurrence, the same day, one layer out:** the totality guard and the `instruction` fix are correct and are in the template, and neither had reached the deployment that was running. `lexgenius-pipeline/adws/adw_modules/code_review.py` has no `_node_goal` function at all, so run `run-0120c32064d144c2aa55c344087e0b0a` had every reviewer handed the derived string — "Make the gate '…' pass over selector '…', changing only the declared outputs" — while the plan it was executing carried the correct instruction in its projected bytes (§19 M13). The chain is now three deep: a per-field patch left a field to find; the class guard that closed that left a *copy* to find. What generalizes is not about projections at all — a lesson is extracted when it reaches the bytes that execute, and this register measures the template. Residual registered as item 50.

45. **A launch that fails before its handle is registered loses its own cause to the quiescence proof.** `Scheduler._run_node` wraps the runner call in `finally: self._quiesce(record, "pre-inventory")`, which is correct and deliberate — classification must not release or block an attempt until its owned process group is proven absent. But `maestro.py`'s quiesce implementation resolves the attempt's handle from a map the runner populates *after* a successful launch, and raises `PROCESS_GROUP_UNTRACKED` when the key is missing at any phase other than `pre-baseline`. A launch that raises before registering a handle — `pane_env_flags` refusing `LAUNCH_REFUSED:SCRATCH_REDIRECT_MISSING` at `launcher.py:137` is the observed instance — therefore hits a `finally` that raises a second exception, and Python replaces the in-flight one with it. The node blocks `QUIESCENCE_UNPROVEN`, which is terminal, and the real cause is gone from the row: the operator is told the harness could not prove a process was absent, about a process that was never started. It also defeats item 42's discharge for exactly this family of failure, since a `LaunchFailed` destroyed in a `finally` never reaches the containment handler that would have classified it. Severity as assessed at registration: P1 — a terminal block on the wrong reason, with the diagnostic the fix in item 42 exists to produce discarded. Why it is registered rather than fixed here: the obvious repair — adding the attempt to the `proven_absent` set whenever a launch raises — **lies** for the refusals raised *after* the pane split, `LAUNCH_REFUSED:SHELL_NOT_READY` and `LAUNCH_REFUSED:NO_PANE` among them, where a pane exists and its process group is exactly what quiescence is for. Marking those proven-absent would make the proof report a fact nobody measured, which is the shape §8.3 refuses on principle. Discharge: the launcher reports, as a typed field on the refusal, whether a pane was created before it failed; quiesce treats no-pane-created as proven-absent by construction and everything else as it does today; and a negative test drives both arms, asserting that a pre-pane refusal reaches the containment handler with its own class intact and that a post-pane refusal still requires a measured absence. Provenance: found 2026-08-18 while tracing why a node whose launch was refused for a missing scratch environment blocked with no launcher class recorded. **Discharged the same day:** every launcher exit after the process split raises `LaunchRefused`, constructed *after* its cleanup has run, so the refusal's `pane_created` is a statement about a completed action rather than a fail-closed default; a pre-pane refusal reaches the containment handler with its own class intact and a post-pane refusal still requires a measured absence. The residual lesson is registered as §19 M3 and bound by §17 item 117: the fail-closed default was not wrong in principle, it was wrong because a frame that *knew* the answer declined to state it.

46. **A deterministic launch refusal is classified LAUNCHER_TRANSIENT and spends two doomed retries.** With item 42 discharged, a refused launch now carries a typed `LauncherFailure` and spends the launcher budget — one or two attempts, zero for CREDENTIAL. That is right for the class the name describes: pane allocation, startup, transport, faults a second attempt can plausibly survive. It is wrong for a refusal that is a property of the configuration or the plan rather than of the moment, `LAUNCH_REFUSED:SCRATCH_REDIRECT_MISSING` being the observed one — a call site that omits an environment omits it identically on every attempt. The node makes two more launches that cannot succeed, then blocks `LAUNCHER_BUDGET_EXHAUSTED`, a reason that says a budget ran out when nothing was ever retryable. The cost is bounded and the diagnosis is misleading, which is the ordering of harms that keeps this at P2 rather than P1. Why it is registered rather than fixed: §7.5 closes the retry classes at three and makes the closure load-bearing, so a fourth class — a deterministic-refusal class with a zero budget — is a design change and is explicitly ruled out as a bug fix. The narrower alternative, extending §7.5's existing non-retryable family (`GATE_NOT_FALSIFIABLE`, `CODE_NODE_NO_EFFECT`, `PERMISSION_SCOPE_VIOLATION`) with launcher refusals that are deterministic by construction, is a change to that family's membership rule and needs the same deliberation. Discharge: a ruling recorded here on which of the two shapes §7.5 takes, and either way a partition of `LauncherFailure` members into retryable and deterministic that the budget function reads, with a negative test asserting a deterministic refusal blocks on its first occurrence with a reason naming the refusal rather than the budget. Provenance: found 2026-08-18 while verifying item 42's discharge against the failure that motivated it. **Discharged:** the ruling this item asked for was taken in favour of the narrower alternative — `DETERMINISTIC_REFUSAL` partitions `LauncherFailure` members within §7.5's existing three classes with a zero retry budget, rather than adding a fourth class — implemented in `retry_policy.py` and `scheduler.py` and driven by `tests/test_refusal_determinism.py` and `tests/test_launcher_classification.py`.

47. **The admission surface check proves two declarations agree, never that either matches the behaviour.** §6.4's surface obligation refuses a plan whose requirement names a path no lane can write, which is what §19 M9 cost a budget to learn. What it establishes is containment between `requirements[].surface` and `extensions.maestro.outputs`, and both are written by one author in one sitting from one mental model. Measured against the incident plan: setting every requirement's surface to exactly its own lane's declared outputs — the surface an author naturally writes from outputs already chosen — admits the plan, and the run burns identically. The converse containment now required (every declared output must appear in some bound requirement's surface, §17 item 122) closes the copying shape, because a copied surface leaves no output unclaimed only when the copy is total, and a total copy is a surface that at least enumerates the real write set. It does **not** close the general case: an author who under-declares *both* fields together — omitting the legacy writers from the outputs and from the surface alike, which is exactly what the incident plan did — satisfies both directions and is admitted. Severity as assessed at registration: P2, because the defect it was built for is now refused and what remains needs an author to be wrong in two places consistently. Discharge: a third statement of the same fact, authored from a different source at a different moment, that the two can be checked against — the source document's own scope for the lane is the obvious candidate and is not currently transcribed into anything typed — or a recorded decision that plan review is the only actor that can catch a consistently under-declared plan, with §13.5's reviewer heuristic naming the question. Provenance: found 2026-08-18 by reproducing the first repair against the plan it was written for, before it shipped.

48. **A false effect disposition is unfalsifiable by any structural check, and exactly one actor ever reads the field.** §6.4's effect obligation refuses a requirement that declares it *performs* an act the plan prohibits, and refuses an act declared `planned` that no requirement ever executes (§17 items 123, 125). Neither can see a requirement that declares `planned` or `fake_only` for an act its module in fact performs against a real client — the disposition is a claim about code that does not exist when the plan is admitted. That would be an ordinary residual, adjudicated by review, except that the projection makes the review surface single. `requirements[]` does not reach `maestro-plan.v1`: measured against the real IR, `surface`, `effects`, `prohibited_effects`, `mutation` and `disposition` are all absent from the projected draft, and the previous run's finalization reviewer input carries no `requirements`, no `oracle`, and no `source_artifacts`. So the plan-contract reviewer at `plan review` is the only independent actor that ever sees a disposition; the finalization reviewer cannot draw a finding on it, and the node reviewer sees only what §17's contract projection carries. Severity as assessed at registration: P2 — one honest review is not nothing, and the acts in question are prohibited plan-wide, so a false `planned` still has to survive a node reviewer looking at code that calls a client. Discharge: either a check with an input the plan does not supply — a post-merge assertion over the merged surface that no module under a `none` or `planned` disposition imports or constructs the corresponding client, which is decidable from the diff and is the first structural falsifier available — or a recorded decision that dispositions are review-adjudicated, with the `plan review` prompt naming them explicitly as the cell to read, since a reviewer that is the only reader ought to be told so. Provenance: found 2026-08-18 by measuring which IR fields survive projection, after §19 M13 made the question worth asking. **Amended 2026-08-18:** the ground this item's P2 rests on — "one honest review is not nothing" — is false as implemented. `plan review` dispatches no model at all; see item 55, and read the two together. **Severity restated 2026-08-19 to P1**, which is the amendment above carried into the number rather than left beside it. P2 priced a residual guarded by one honest review; there is no review, so nothing guards it: a requirement may declare `planned` or `fake_only` for an act its module in fact performs against a real client, and no actor reads the disposition before the run. The node reviewer remains the only reader of anything downstream, and it sees code rather than the disposition that was supposed to constrain it. Leaving P2 in place while the sentence beneath it said the opposite is itself item 51's failure mode — a register recording a closure it has already disproved. The structural falsifier this item's first discharge option describes (a post-merge assertion that no module under a `none` or `planned` disposition imports or constructs the corresponding client) is **not built**; that option is now the only discharge, since the second — review adjudication — has no reader to assign it to.

49. **Runner capability is proven for one runner and refused for the other, and a runner that fails mid-run is still indistinguishable from bad work.** §17 item 121's probe is measured for pytest: capable interpreters exit `NO_TESTS_COLLECTED`, an interpreter carrying the wrong dependency set exits `USAGE_ERROR`, an absent module exits 1, all three measured on the machine that produced §19 M8. `Gate.runner` also admits `vitest`, whose probe argv and pass condition are **unmeasured** — no vitest exists on the machine where this was written. The design's answer is to refuse a runner with no measured probe row, which is fail-closed and correct, and it means the vitest gate path is refused rather than covered: a repository that would use it cannot run until someone measures the row. Separately, the preflight bounds only the run's start. A runner that resolves and probes capable, and whose environment then changes mid-run — a virtual environment rebuilt, an interpreter upgraded under a symlink — produces unparseable gate output, classifies ENVIRONMENTAL, and spends the environmental budget on identical retries, which is §19 M8's original shape surviving inside the window the preflight does not cover. That residual is deliberate: a runner that genuinely broke mid-run *is* environmental, and the budget is the right container. Severity as assessed at registration: P2 for the vitest gap (a refused path, not a wrong answer) and P2 for the mid-run case (correct classification, wasted budget). Discharge: a measured vitest probe row with the same three-way evidence pytest has; and, for the mid-run case, a recorded decision on whether the probe re-runs on the first unparseable gate of a run — which would convert two wasted attempts into one typed refusal — or whether the environmental budget is accepted as the bound. Provenance: registered 2026-08-18 at the design of item 121, before implementation, so the unmeasured row is a stated gap rather than a discovered one.

50. **Three runtime copies exist, a test binds two of them, and the deployment is reconciled by hand.** `tests/test_template_parity.py` compares this repository's template against the-library's copy file by file, in both directions, and it is the reason the 6009-line loss mode cannot recur between those two. `lexgenius-pipeline/adws/` is covered by nothing. Measured 2026-08-18: twelve modules and seventeen test modules differ from the template, `handoff_budget.py` is absent, and — the divergence that had a cost — `code_review.py` carries no `_node_goal`, so §19 M1's reviewer-contract fix had never executed anywhere and the run this session repairs was reviewed against a placeholder (§19 M13). `diff -rq` had been reporting that filename for as long as the divergence existed; what it does not report is what the divergence *does*, and nobody reads a filename as "every reviewer in this deployment is judging against a derived goal". Severity as assessed at registration: P1 — it silently degraded the most load-bearing semantic judgment in the design, across a whole run, with every test in every repository green. Why it is registered rather than fixed: a deployment carries its own `maestro.config.yaml`, its own data directories, and legitimately runs ahead of or behind the template, so the parity test's equality rule cannot simply be pointed at it; deciding what a deployment must match, and when, is a design question this round is not taking. Discharge: a check the deployment itself runs at `run start` — the cheapest honest form is a refusal when the installed reviewer cannot carry a node's instruction, which is one symbol lookup and states its own meaning (§17 item 126) — plus a rule that a template change with a named behavioural cost records that cost where the deployments are reconciled, rather than leaving `diff -rq` to imply it. Provenance: found 2026-08-18 by reading the reviewer prompt a blocked run had already written, after its findings looked narrower than the defect they were about. **Note, 2026-08-18:** `lexgenius-pipeline/adws/` was subsequently brought level by hand — `diff -rq` against the template now reports one differing tracked file, `maestro.config.yaml`, which is deployment-specific by design, and `_node_goal` is present. The item stays **open**, because nothing enforced that and nothing would notice it drifting again; the measurement above is what the deployment looked like before the reconciliation, and it is kept for that reason.

51. **The dead-seam allowlist is one-directional, so an entry outlives the defect it names.** `tests/test_no_dead_seams.py` converts "nobody calls this" into a named work item: a symbol with production readers and no production writers must appear in `ALLOWED` with a reason, and the file's own text says an entry marked `DEFERRED` "is a work item with a name, not a suppression". The check fails when such a symbol is **missing** from the allowlist. Nothing fails when an allowlisted symbol **acquires** a production caller, so the entry remains, still asserting a defect that has been fixed. Observed 2026-08-18: `lifecycle.record_result`, `lifecycle.audit_results` and `verification.adjudicate_result` each still carry "DEFERRED D7: … no production writer" / "never called in production" after `scheduler.py::_record_result` began writing results on the settle path. Item 43 was written from those entries and went stale with them, which is the concrete cost: this register's own accuracy depends on a mechanism that cannot notice its work items being completed. Severity as assessed at registration: P2 — it produces false open items rather than false green tests, and a false open item is the failure mode §16.3 exists to avoid in itself. Discharge: make the allowlist bidirectional — an entry whose symbol has acquired a production caller fails the test, naming the entry to delete — which costs one assertion over data the test already computes, and audit the current entries once against production callers when it lands. Provenance: found 2026-08-18 while re-verifying item 43 during a register reconciliation, which is the only reason it was found at all.

52. **An approved artifact whose approval can no longer be re-derived.** `requirements[].surface`, `requirements[].effects` and `extensions.maestro.prohibited_effects` were made required from their first version rather than optional, on §3.6 B8's ground that a field added later is optional forever. The cost is precise and was measured rather than predicted: the approved plan `cmo-consolidation-l` — the one this session's incidents were found in — now **fails** the validator that approved it. `planctl validate` returns exit 1 with fifteen errors against it: `extensions.maestro missing: prohibited_effects`, and `requirements[0..13] missing: effects, surface`, all fourteen. Its receipt is not thereby invalid: the IR bytes are unchanged, they still hash to `390619d5e9917e7562df600a1c473b90fcebc8b608f9286763a6ca61625d1511`, and that is exactly the `ir_sha256` the receipt binds, with its HMAC intact. What is gone is **reproducibility** — the receipt says a validator returned PASS on those bytes, and no validator that exists now will return PASS on them again. Severity as assessed at registration: P2. It reaches no run, because the plan is superseded and its projected `maestro-plan.v1` carries its own finalization receipt; it reaches an auditor, who finds an approval that cannot be re-derived and has nothing in the document telling them that is expected. Why it is registered rather than fixed: the alternative was an optional field, which B8 forbids for exactly the reason that made these fields worth adding, so this is an accepted cost and not a defect — but an accepted cost that is nowhere written down is indistinguishable from an unnoticed one, which is the failure mode §16.3 exists for. This will recur on every future field that becomes required. Discharge, if the class is ever worth closing: a receipt that records the validator's own identity — `validator_sha256` is already in the receipt shape — read by an audit path that reports "approved under validator X, not reproducible under current validator Y" rather than leaving a reader to discover the difference by running it. Provenance: measured 2026-08-18 while verifying that the schema change had closed the planctl/ingress deadlock, against the real approved plan rather than a probe.

53. **Two codebases share one vocabulary and nothing checks that they agree.** The effect names, the dispositions and the surface mutation kinds exist twice: as JSON Schema enums in `the-library/skills/plan-contract/schemas/plan-ir-v1.schema.json` (`$defs.effectName`, `$defs.effectDisposition`, `$defs.requirementSurfaceEntry.mutation`) and as Python constants in the Maestro template (`plan_validate.EFFECTS`, `DISPOSITIONS`, `MUTATIONS`). Verified equal on 2026-08-18, element for element and in the same order, by loading both and comparing. Nothing enforces it. A sixth effect added to one side and not the other, or a rename applied to one, reproduces the original planctl/ingress deadlock — an IR that one side accepts and the other refuses — with every test in both repositories green while it happens. The mirrored well-formedness checks make this sharper rather than softer: planctl deliberately reproduces the ingress refusals so an author sees them while the plan is still authorable, which is right, and is only safe while the two vocabularies are pinned to each other. Severity as assessed at registration: P2 — it produces a deadlock rather than a wrong answer, and the deadlock is loud. This is **item 50's class with a different pair of artifacts**: there, three runtime copies with a test binding two; here, two vocabulary definitions with a test binding none. The analysis of why discipline is not a mechanism is written there and is not repeated. Discharge, and it is landing: a test in either repository that loads the peer's schema JSON and asserts the three enums equal its own constants, skipping only when the peer is not checked out — the shape `tests/test_template_parity.py` already uses for the runtime copies. Provenance: found 2026-08-18 while verifying the schema change against the real plan; the equality held, and the absence of anything asserting it did not.

54. **The consolidation plan carries thirteen verifiers against a reviewable target of twelve, and that is accepted rather than resolved.** `planctl validate` emits `plan.scope_advisory` on both `cmo-consolidation-l` and `cmo-consolidation-l-r2`: "verifiers count 13 is above the reviewable target of 12; a reviewer that has to sample this plan cannot establish its claims." It is a warning by design, it blocks nothing, and it has never been acted on. One lane above the threshold is the smallest possible exceedance, and the alternative — splitting Phase 1 into its own plan — costs a second authoring, review and finalization cycle for a plan whose lanes are already sequenced by `depends_on`. Severity as assessed at registration: P3. What is being accepted is not a defect in the plan but a bound on the plan reviewer's coverage: at thirteen the reviewer samples rather than establishes, so the plan-review cell that §16.3 item 48 identifies as the *only* independent reader of `surface` and `effects` is itself sampling. Those two items compound and are worth reading together. Discharge: either split the plan at a phase boundary, or raise the target with a stated reason for the new number, or record per-plan that the advisory was seen and accepted by name — which is what this item does for this plan and nothing more. Provenance: observed 2026-08-18 in the validator output of the interlock verification, on both plans, unchanged by any work this session.

55. **The only independent reader of `surface` and `effects` does not read.** Item 48 registers the plan-contract reviewer at `plan review` as the single actor that ever sees a disposition, and prices the residual at P2 on the ground that one honest review is not nothing. That ground is false as implemented. `maestro.py::_plan_review` runs exactly two subprocesses — `planctl review`, then `planctl validate --require-approved` — and stamps `reviewer.id` and `reviewer.vendor` from configuration into the receipt it writes. No model is dispatched and no pane is opened; the `reviewer:` configuration block's model, profile and timeouts drive the **node** reviewer during a run, not this verb. What the receipt carries is real and is not weakened by this: it is signed, it binds the IR bytes by `ir_sha256`, and its HMAC verifies. What it does not carry is a reading. The failure is therefore one of belief rather than of mechanism, and the belief is written down: `docs/plan-authoring.md` instructs an author to direct the plan reviewer's attention to the plan's surface and effects, which a reader takes as evidence that a semantic read happens, and nothing between that instruction and the receipt performs one. Severity as assessed at registration: P2 against the code, which does what it was built to do, and **P1 against item 48 and the runbook**, both of which credit this verb with a judgment it never makes — a false closure in a register whose purpose is to prevent them, which is item 51's failure mode with a different mechanism. Discharge: either dispatch a model at `plan review` against the authored IR with the dispositions named as the cells to read — which is what item 48's second discharge option assumed already existed — or restate item 48's severity and the runbook's instruction on the fact that `surface` and `effects` have **no** independent reader before a run, and move the falsifier to the post-merge assertion item 48's first option describes. Provenance: found 2026-08-18 by reading `_plan_review`'s implementation while landing §19 M14, after the runbook's wording implied a reader that the code does not contain. **Discharged 2026-08-19 by the second option, in the half that was a false closure.** `docs/plan-authoring.md` step 4 now states plainly that no model is dispatched, that the verb takes the reviewer's configured identity and vendor as strings and signs a receipt bound to the IR bytes by `ir_sha256`, and that `surface` and `effects` have no independent reader before a run begins — so the runbook no longer instructs an author to direct an attention that does not exist. Item 48's severity is restated to P1 above. Both halves of the false closure are closed. What is **not** done, and is not claimed: the falsifier has not moved. Item 48's post-merge assertion is unbuilt, so a false disposition still has no structural check anywhere — the difference is that the register and the runbook now say so instead of crediting a review that never happens. The residual is unchanged in size and honest in its description, which is the whole of what this item asked for.

56. **A FAIL finalization receipt is permanent and B10's operator escape does not exist.** `finalization.finalize` short-circuits on `store.has(plan_digest)`, receipts are create-once, and replay is keyed on the digest alone — all three deliberate (§6.5). Nothing revokes, supersedes, or sets aside a receipt: the verb surface has no such escape and neither module contains one. So a plan that FAILs is unrunnable at those bytes forever, `run start` refusing it with `RUN_RECEIPT_NOT_PASS` on every future invocation, **including after the cause of the FAIL has been fixed in Maestro itself**. §19 M16 is that case: a projection defect condemned a correct plan, and the only route back was a full re-authoring cycle whose entire purpose was to change the digest. §3.6 B10 requires that re-review of byte-identical input be impossible or explicitly recorded *with an operator escape*; the first half is implemented and the second is not, which is why this is registered rather than treated as by-design. The asymmetry that a discharge has to respect: a FAIL caused by the plan must stay terminal, or reviewers become re-rollable until one passes, which is the failure the create-once rule exists to prevent. A FAIL caused by the rubric, the projection, or the reviewer harness is a fact about Maestro, and Maestro's own defects should not permanently condemn an operator's plan. Severity as assessed at registration: P2 — the workaround exists and is mechanical, but it costs an authoring cycle and it silently makes `plan_id` load-bearing for correctness, which it was never meant to be. Discharge: an escape that records why the receipt was set aside, retains the original in the store rather than deleting it, and admits a fresh review of the same digest — the shape `FINALIZATION_STALLED` already establishes, which writes no receipt and permits a rerun; this is the case where a verdict was written but the machine produced it. Filed as issue #38. Provenance: found 2026-08-19 when `run start cmo-consolidation-l-r3` was refused and the FAIL proved to be Maestro's, not the plan's. **Discharged 2026-08-19, in the shape this item specified.** `ReceiptStore.set_aside` retains the FAILed receipt and its original signature under an archival name, writes a signed `SetAsideRecord` naming the invoker, the reason, and the sha256 of the exact receipt bytes it superseded, and frees the live slot; the next `finalize` then reviews those bytes afresh by the same path a `FINALIZATION_STALLED` rerun takes — **the absence of a receipt**, never a decision that reads the record, so §1.2 is respected and the reason stays an audit field nothing consumes. `finalize` itself is unchanged and the replay key is unchanged, so every receipt nobody set aside still short-circuits on the digest alone, PASS or FAIL. The asymmetry this item named is what the escape's refusals implement: it needs the finalizer's signing key, refuses a PASS, refuses a digest with no receipt, refuses a blank invoker or reason, appends a permanent signed record visible in the store forever, and is called by nothing in Maestro — reachable only from `maestro plan set-aside`, so deciding whether a given FAIL was the plan's or the machine's stays a judgment the verb makes *attributable* rather than a judgment the verb makes. `maestro plan set-aside-log` reads the escapes back with the receipt each one superseded, both signature-verified. `tests/test_receipt_set_aside.py` drives all of it; §17 item 132 binds it. **One residual, registered rather than fixed:** a set-aside digest and a never-finalized one are the same fact to the ledger, which has no knowledge of the escape at all — the store can tell them apart through `set_aside_records`, and `run start` now says which through the typed `cause` field on `RUN_RECEIPT_ABSENT`, but `plan list` reports a set-aside digest as unfinalized and nothing in the run path reads the record for any other purpose. *(Moot since 2026-08-20: `finalize` writes only PASS receipts, §6.5; a FAIL receipt can exist only from the earlier reviewer path, and item 132 records its escape.)*

57. **Grade deflation has no structural bound, and the honest finding is that no cheap one exists.** Rubric-stamped severity makes verdict *inflation* impossible: the reviewer cannot emit a severity or a verdict, so it cannot escalate an ADVISORY check into a rejection (§6.5). The graded axis (§19 M17) is deliberately not symmetric with that, and the asymmetry is the exposure. A reviewer that finds a genuine ERROR and grades it `warning` — with a plausible `grade_rationale` — lets the diff through, and nothing in the design contradicts it. Two candidate detectors were assessed and neither is built. **(a) A grade that contradicts its own rationale is not detectable structurally, and this is stated plainly rather than left as future work.** Deciding whether "this swallows every `HeadObject` error" justifies `warning` rather than `error` is a semantic judgment over free text; the only instrument that could make it is another model reading the first one's prose, which is reviewer-of-reviewer — the construction §6.5 already refuses for chunked plans — and it would key a consequence on model prose, which §1.2 forbids outright. There is no cheap structural version of this and there is unlikely to be an expensive one either. **(b) The distribution is observable and the data already exists, but building the instrument does not create its reader.** §19 M12 is the precedent: an operator reading a field's disposition census on its first use found it implausible, and that census *was* the detection mechanism. The equivalent here is a count of (check_id × grade) per reviewer identity, and every input is already on disk and already signed — the receipt carries each cell's grade, its threshold, and the reviewer's (route, model, session id), and the ledger beside it carries each grade's rationale. So the cost of the *data* is zero and the cost of the *verb* is a reader over the receipt store, perhaps eighty lines. What it would catch is a population fact: a reviewer that never emits `error` at all across N reviews, or an `error` rate that collapses after a route or model change. What it would **not** catch is the case this item is about — one deflated finding on one diff, which is invisible in any distribution. And a report nobody is obliged to read is decoration, which is B15's rule pointed the other way; M12's census worked because an operator happened to read it, and a verb does not manufacture that operator. Severity as assessed at registration: **P2**, and lower than it looks, for two reasons stated so that a later reader does not re-derive them. The exposure is bounded on one side already: severity remains code's, so the *worst* a deflation achieves is a merge with a recorded finding, never an ADVISORY check promoted or a FAIL suppressed into silence. And it is **recoverable rather than merely unprevented** — a deflated finding is still written to the ledger and still carried in the signed receipt with its grade, under the subject digest of the node that merged, so the evidence of every deflation survives the merge it permitted and an audit after the fact is a read rather than an investigation. Discharge, whenever someone commits to the reading: the distribution verb of (b), reported per reviewer identity and per check, with a stated expectation of what a plausible shape looks like — because a census with no prior is a number, not a detector. Cross-vendor grade agreement and a periodic operator sweep were both considered and are **out of scope by design decision, not by oversight**: the first doubles every review's cost to bound a failure nobody has yet observed, and the second is the unbounded-loop shape A9 convicts. Provenance: assessed 2026-08-19 while closing §19 M17's residuals; registered rather than built, on the finding above.
