Every checkout is pinned to the accepted candidate SHA or the read-only declared base. The canonical acceptance manifest records the complete repository-to-SHA map. Global gates run in declaration order, from acceptance-relative working directories, as `(gate.runner,) + gate.argv`; each requires exit zero and at least `min_cases`. Gate results become immutable store rows, so a resumed coordinator does not rerun an already recorded external check. Cleanup removes only the exact acceptance worktree registrations and paths; candidate worktrees remain recovery evidence.

Repository outcomes are `ACCEPTED`, `BLOCKED`, or `CANCELLED`. The workspace outcome is the corresponding total terminal declaration:

- `ACCEPTED`: every repository accepted and every global gate passed;
- `BLOCKED`: a participant, binding, ancestry proof, acceptance composition, or global gate failed, with durable reasons and blocked descendants;
- `CANCELLED`: cancellation was requested and no active or pending repository remains.

Partial success is never renamed acceptance.

### 18.6 Ledger, leases, and restart

`adw_modules/coordinator_store.py` is the sole durable authority for workspace bytes/digest, repository declarations and state, child/candidate identities, accepted SHAs, global-gate results, cancellation, outcome, publication intent/steps, audit transitions, and coordinator lease. Production coordinator code performs no direct SQL and launches no repository command itself.

One expiring lease excludes concurrent coordinators. The current owner heartbeats and may release only its own lease; another owner may take over only after expiry. Restart reloads the exact stored workspace rather than re-projecting current files. An inherited `RUNNING` row first adjudicates an existing strict result; otherwise it resumes the exact registered candidate and child identity. Accepted repositories and recorded gates are not relaunched. Acceptance worktrees are reconstructible transient state and are safely reclaimed before an exact rebuild. Audit rows are append-only observations, never execution authority.

### 18.7 Publication is a persisted compare-and-swap protocol

Publication begins only after workspace `ACCEPTED`. `CoordinatorStore.prepare_publication()` persists the complete ordered target vector, including each repository's expected base, target branch, accepted SHA, remote, and candidate branch, before any target mutation.

For `local_refs`, all target refs are preflighted against their persisted expected bases. Each update uses `git update-ref <target> <accepted> <expected>` in vector order. A later failure rolls back every earlier update with the inverse compare-and-swap, but only while that ref still names the accepted SHA. A rollback race yields `MANUAL_RECOVERY_REQUIRED`; it is never overwritten.

For `pull_requests`, preflight covers every writable repository before the durable intent: repository identity, target head, candidate remote state, remote configuration, and `gh auth status`. Publishing then pushes each exact candidate with force-with-lease semantics and creates its PR. Remote PR creation is not transactional. Once any external mutation succeeds, a later failure yields `PARTIALLY_PUBLISHED` and then `MANUAL_RECOVERY_REQUIRED`; Maestro does not claim remote rollback.

There is no distributed Git transaction across unrelated repositories. Local compare-and-swap plus bounded rollback detects interference; it does not make independent repositories atomic.

### 18.8 Public operator flow

The executable surface is exactly:

```text
maestro workspace validate
maestro workspace finalize
maestro workspace start
maestro workspace status
maestro workspace cancel
maestro workspace resume
maestro workspace publish
maestro workspace rollback
```

`validate` proves canonical bytes and the complete typed workspace. `finalize` verifies every child PASS receipt and writes or replays the signed workspace receipt. `start` verifies that receipt before graph projection; `resume` reopens the same durable run id and identities. `status` reports store authority, including repositories, gates, outcome, cancellation, and publication. `cancel` records intent before active participants are boundedly cancelled. `publish` consumes the accepted run's persisted publication vector. `rollback --db --manifest-dir --run-id --actor` resumes the persisted publication rollback using that same vector. Every invocation emits one machine-readable JSON object. `start` and `resume` return nonzero unless they reach `ACCEPTED`; the other verbs return nonzero on refusal.

### 18.9 Negative guarantees

- No repository head, working tree, current branch, or unsigned manifest is reconstructed as authority after projection.
- No read-only repository executes a participant or receives a candidate branch.
- No child process can authorize another repository, workspace, plan digest, or base commit.
- No coordinator recovery invents a new attempt identity over an existing `RUNNING` row.
- No global gate runs against mutable candidate worktrees or unaccepted SHAs.
- No publication starts before all writable targets pass preflight.
- No failed remote operation is reported as rolled back unless an exact local compare-and-swap proved that rollback.
- No multi-repository feature changes the single-repository plan schema, receipt verdict, retry accounting, worktree bracket, or merge proof.

---

## 19. Maestro production incidents

§3 is archaeology performed read-only against another system. This section is the same discipline turned on Maestro itself: failures observed in the Maestro runtime during real runs against a real repository, recorded in the same form — what failed, the evidence, its status, and the lesson that binds future work. It is kept separate from §3 deliberately. §3's rows are frozen against Strav at `addf9ca` and its verification legend is scoped to that tree; mixing Maestro's own incidents into it would destroy both the freeze and the legend. Where a row here is a recurrence of a §3 row in Maestro's own code, it says so, because a lesson that was extracted and then recurred is stronger evidence than a lesson observed once.

The provenance column matters as much as the failure. A defect found by a test is a defect the design anticipated the shape of. A defect found because a production run died on it is a defect the design did not anticipate, and its lesson is correspondingly load-bearing — five of the first seven rows below are of the second kind. A third provenance appears in the later rows and is worth naming separately: a defect found by reading an artifact a run had already written, after the run had blocked for some other reason. Those cost no budget of their own.

This section and §16.3 are deliberately not merged, on the same ground §16.3 and §16.5 are not merged. §16.3 registers what the design knew it had **not proven**, item by item, and an item leaves it by being discharged. §19 records what a run **observed**, and what the observation taught — a lesson survives here after its defect is fixed, because the lesson is the durable part. Where a row here corresponds to a register item, both say so: M1 to item 44, M3 to item 45. M1 is the case that justifies keeping the two apart. Item 44 was discharged correctly by its own terms and the discharge was a one-field fix, so the register showed a closed item while the defect class stayed open and produced M1 a day later. A register of unproven predicates cannot catch that; a corpus of observed failures can.

### 19.1 Incidents of 2026-08-18

| ID | Failure | Evidence | Found by | Lesson that binds future work |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| M1 | **Silent field loss across a projection.** `PlanNode` carried no `instruction` field and `Plan.to_plan_nodes` projected the node field by field, so the instruction never crossed. `code_review.py` read it as `getattr(node, "instruction", "")` and therefore always received `""`, and `_fallback_instruction` supplied its placeholder — "make the gate pass" — to the reviewer of every agent node in every run that has ever run. The reviewer never received the node's goal, its `produces`, its acceptance, or its `min_cases`. | The reviewer contract of §3.6 B9 was degenerate in production while the whole suite was green, because no test compared the projection's output against the source's declared fields. This is the **second** instance of the shape: `min_cases` was dropped by the same projection earlier and was fixed as a one-field patch (§16.3 item 44), which is why `instruction` was still there to be found. | A production run whose review found nothing, read back against the handoff actually written | A hand-written projection between two representations of one fact is RC1's shape at function scope, and every such projection is total or it is a latent defect. The fix is a **class-level totality guard** that compares source and target field sets by name *and* every projected instance by value — not another per-field patch. A per-field patch leaves the next field to be discovered the same way this one was. A `getattr` with a default over a field that is supposed to always exist is the same defect wearing a seatbelt: it converts a missing field into a plausible empty value, and an empty contract is indistinguishable from a permissive one. |
| M2 | **A guard whose universe came from the filesystem.** `worktree.py::inventory()` enumerated the attempt worktree with `os.walk`, under a comment that stated the intent outright — "No excludes and no ignore list". §8.3's write-permission check consumed that inventory, counted **16,090 gitignored `.venv/**` paths** as writes outside the node's declared globs, and discarded an attempt that had run **209 turns** of real work. | The conviction was unappealable because every path in it was real: the files existed, the agent's toolchain had created them, and the check's arithmetic was correct. Bounded now to `git ls-files --cached --others --exclude-standard`. The unbounded `offending_paths` join was bounded in the same change — the defect that made the check wrong also made its own evidence unreadable. | A production run: a 209-turn attempt discarded at the permission check | A guard over *what an attempt wrote* must draw its universe from the same authority that will later decide what is committed. A guard whose universe is the filesystem will convict on artifacts git itself ignores, and it will do so with true evidence, which is what makes it survive review. Whenever a check enumerates a working tree, name the enumerating authority in the check's own text and make it git. Second rule, learned in the same hour: evidence rendered into a failure message is bounded at the point of rendering, always — an unbounded join is how a check that is merely wrong becomes a check nobody can diagnose. |
| M3 | **A cleaned-up failure path that did not say so in its type.** Every launcher exit after the process split raised an untyped `HerdrCallError`. `LaunchFailed.pane_created` had no statement to read, so it fell back to its fail-closed default of `True`; the scheduler then demanded quiescence proof for a pane handle that had never been registered; `PROCESS_GROUP_UNTRACKED` was the only answer available, and it converted a retryable launch failure into terminal `QUIESCENCE_UNPROVEN`. | The cleanup was correct — the pane really had been torn down — and the run failed terminally *because* of it. Registered open as §16.3 item 45 and discharged the same day: every post-split exit now raises `LaunchRefused`, constructed **after** cleanup has run, so the type is a statement about a completed action rather than a hope. | A production run that died terminally on a failure that should have retried | Fail-closed defaults are correct only where the caller genuinely cannot know. A path that has *already* cleaned up after itself must say so **in its type**, because a conservative default downstream is then guaranteed to be wrong on exactly the paths that were handled best. State the fact where it is known, at the moment it becomes true; never leave a downstream default to guess at a fact an upstream frame held. Same shape as §3.6 B14 one layer down: an outcome that cannot be expressed is an outcome that will be misreported. |
| M4 | **"Unobserved" collapsed into "did not happen."** One terminal code, `AGENT_PROMPT_UNSUBMITTED`, covered both *the pane's revision counter could not be read* and *the pane's revision counter did not move*. A momentary failure to read the instrument retired an attempt that would have succeeded on retry. | Split into `AGENT_PROMPT_UNOBSERVED` — transient, retryable, a property of the observer — and `AGENT_PROMPT_UNSUBMITTED` — terminal, a property of the agent. | Test failures on the launcher's own fakes, once the fakes were made to carry `revision` | "I could not read the meter" and "the meter did not move" are different facts and must never share an outcome code or a retry class. Wherever a lifecycle decision reads an instrument, the instrument's own failure is a **third** outcome, and a classifier carrying only two will retire healthy work at whatever rate the instrument is flaky. This generalizes §7.5: retry class is a property of *why the observation ended*, not of what the observation said. |
| M5 | **Run state that existed only because a live process remembered to write it.** `latest_outcome` had exactly one writer, `Scheduler._declare`. A scheduler that exited without declaring — killed, crashed, or cancelled through `_run_cancel`, which never touched the column — left its run reading live forever. `run list` reported **two runs dead at 0 turns** as still running, indefinitely. | Run state is now **derived** from node states plus scheduler liveness, with a new `ABANDONED` state for a run whose scheduler is gone and whose nodes are not terminal. Nothing writes the run's outcome as a fact of its own. | A production run: `run list` showing dead runs as live | A run's state must be a **function of durable facts already recorded for other reasons**, never a value that exists only because a live process remembered to write it. Any state whose single writer lives inside the process whose death that state is supposed to report is, by construction, unwritten in exactly the case that matters. Read §7.3's state set as a derivation over node rows and liveness, not as a column somebody maintains — and when a new terminal condition appears, it is a new derivation, not a new writer. |
| M6 | **A fail-closed preflight installed on a path rather than at a chokepoint.** §3.6 B13's handoff size check was placed on one launch path. A second route reached the same agent without crossing it, so the guarantee "every LLM input is size-checked before dispatch" held for some dispatches. | Moved to `HerdrLauncher.launch`, above the process split, which every route crosses. | Reading the launch paths while fixing M3, before any run hit it | A fail-closed preflight belongs at the narrowest chokepoint **every** caller must cross, and "every caller" is measured *after* the last branch, not before it. A check installed on a path silently loses coverage every time a route is added, and nothing fails when it does — the guarantee decays without a single test going red. When a check's coverage is a claim about the call graph, place it where the call graph makes the claim true by construction. |
| M7 | **An observer that cached the thing whose deletion it was supposed to report.** The visualizer opened the ledger with `immutable=1`, so SQLite never re-read the file and deleted runs kept rendering as live panes; `MaestroRunDetail.vue` compounded it by never clearing `run` on a 404, so a detail view outlived its subject. | Both fixed. The visualizer is the operator's only view of run liveness, so a stale view here is indistinguishable from the M5 defect it would otherwise have surfaced. | An operator looking at a run that had been deleted | A read-only view of a live ledger must be read-only in *permission*, never in the sense of *will not change*. `immutable=1` is a statement about the file, and the file is not immutable. More generally: a diagnostic surface must not be able to fail in the same direction as the defect it exists to reveal — M5 and M7 were both "dead run reads as live", from opposite ends, and either one alone would have masked the other. |
| M8 | **A gate runner resolved from ambient state.** The collection command is built as `("pytest", "--collect-only", "-q", "-o", "addopts=")` and dispatched with no environment override; `launch_env` likewise begins from `dict(os.environ)` and redirects seven cache variables, none of them `PATH`. Which interpreter judges a lane is therefore whatever the shell that typed `run start` happened to resolve. On the incident machine that was a Homebrew pytest carrying none of the repository's dependencies: it could not import the suite's `conftest.py`, collected zero cases, and every lane failed `min_cases` as though the work were bad. | Exit codes cannot separate the two conditions. Measured: a broken interpreter against an existing test file exits **4**, and a correct interpreter against a not-yet-written test file also exits **4** — both `USAGE_ERROR`, both zero cases. The discriminator is a probe decoupled from the plan's selectors, walking the tree while selecting nothing: a capable interpreter exits **5** (`NO_TESTS_COLLECTED`, a documented member of `pytest.ExitCode`) after importing every conftest; an incapable one exits 4, and an absent one exits 1. Measured across three interpreters on the incident machine. | Reading the collector's dispatch while auditing a blocked run, before the next run hit it | The interpreter that executes a gate is an input to the acceptance predicate and must be **declared and probed**, never inherited. A runner taken from the environment makes "did this lane's work pass" a function of the shell that started the run, with nothing in the ledger recording which one answered. Two rules follow. Resolve it once, at a chokepoint, into a type the executors accept in place of a bare command, so a new dispatch site cannot construct an unresolved one. And prove capability with a probe whose signal is an exit code the runner documents — never by reading stderr, and never from the gate's own exit code, which is ambiguous between a broken runner and a selector the lane has not built yet. |
| M9 | **A plan admitted a lane whose contract no attempt could satisfy.** `lane-p1-freeze-and-run-log` carried a requirement for behaviour over the legacy CMO writers while declaring only a new module and its test as outputs. `postgres_writer.py`, `cmo_repo.py`, and `scraper_connector.py` appeared in the declared outputs of none of the plan's fourteen lanes, so no attempt could write the files the behaviour needed, §8.3's permission delta would have rejected the write had one been attempted, and every attempt produced an out-of-contract workaround the reviewer correctly rejected. The node exhausted its review budget on a task no correct attempt could pass. | No relation the IR carried could see the gap: the lane's fixtures, claim subjects, verifier argv, and hash-pinned sources were every one of them satisfied. The only statement that the legacy writers were in scope was prose, and an admission decision keyed on prose is what §1.2 forbids. The first repair was a declared `surface` of `{path, mutation}` records checked for containment — and it was **insufficient in a way worth recording**: an author who declares the surface as exactly the outputs already chosen satisfies it trivially. Reproduced against the incident plan: with each requirement's surface set to its own lane's outputs, the plan is admitted and the run burns identically. Closed by adding the converse containment — every declared output must appear in the surface of some requirement the lane binds. | A production run: two lanes blocked `REVIEW_BUDGET_EXHAUSTED`, eleven unreachable behind them | Satisfiability is an admission obligation, not a review finding. A node whose declared behaviour needs a write surface the plan grants nobody is refusable before a run exists, and refusing it there costs one validation instead of a budget. The second lesson is about the shape of the check rather than its subject: a consistency check between two fields **one author writes in one sitting** is not evidence, because the cheapest way to satisfy it is to copy one into the other. A containment check earns its keep only when it is bidirectional, or when the two sides are authored at different moments from different sources. |
| M10 | **A requirement that both prohibited and prescribed the same effect.** `req-p1-canonical-object-key` declared the module "a pure derivation and policy module with injected clients" and that "no production migration, object mutation, or backfill execution is authorized", while the same requirement instructed it to "verify and hash it first and then server-side copy it to the canonical key". Both readings were defensible. The builder resolved it toward execution and wrote an S3 materializer; the reviewer, judging that materializer on its merits, found a real TOCTOU and a real immutability breach inside it. Three attempts, whole budget, node blocked. | Both reviewer findings were correct — they are defects of executing code whose contract claimed it was pure. The source document resolves the contradiction and the plan lost the resolution in transcription: §L's Phase 1 Exit is that "an import dry-run produces only deterministic decisions", and the copy executes in Phase 2, which a **different lane** already declared. The effect was specified twice and executed by neither reading consistently. | A production run: three attempts, each rejected on a different defect inside work the contract should never have authorised | An authorisation boundary is a typed field or it is not a boundary. Prose can hold a prohibition and a prescription in the same paragraph and read as sensible; a closed enum cannot. Where a plan's source document draws a boundary — what this work may and may not cause — transcribe it into declared values checked at admission, and let the prose keep only what the values already fix. The tell that this defect is present is a builder and a reviewer disagreeing about what the node was for, each with the text on their side. |
| M11 | **A typed vocabulary that named mechanisms where the source document prohibited acts.** The effect enum introduced for M10 was named after transports — `s3_object_write`, `network_retrieval`, `dynamodb_write`, `database_migration` — while §L prohibits acts: object mutation, backfill execution, migration. A lane can use a transport without committing the act, so cells became unfalsifiable. `req-p6-parity-harness` reads an HTTP API and backfills nothing; both `fake_only` and `none` were defensible for `network_retrieval`, which is to say neither was checkable. The same latency sat in the others: an alembic revision authored is not a migration executed, and a report artifact written to S3 is not a canonical object materialised. | Renamed to name the act — `canonical_object_write`, `source_backfill`, `catalog_projection_write`, `source_object_delete`, `migration_execution` — with each entry carrying the source document's own words as a required `meaning`, read by the plan reviewer and rendered into the node's contract so both reviewers resolve a cell from the same sentence. Re-deriving the incident plan under the act names flipped a second cell nobody had questioned. | An authoring agent overriding a grid it had been handed, and being right | A closed vocabulary must name the thing the authority prohibits, not the machinery that would carry it out. Naming the mechanism admits lanes that use it innocently and forces their authors to choose a wrong word, and a wrong word in a typed field is worse than prose because it looks checked. Two corollaries. Where the vocabulary is derived from a source document, carry that document's own words beside each member, or authors and reviewers will resolve the same cell from different texts. And when an executing agent contradicts a specification it was handed, that disagreement is evidence about the specification — this row exists because one was escalated instead of corrected. |
| M12 | **A closed vocabulary with no way to say "not applicable."** The disposition enum shipped as `performed`, `planned`, `fake_only`, with a rule that every requirement state a disposition for every prohibited effect. Exhaustiveness was deliberate — silence is how the M9 defect passes — but the enum offered no member for an effect a lane simply does not touch, so authors reached for `planned`. On first use, 51 of 65 entries were `planned` and **3 of those 51 were truthful**. Two entire columns were false end to end: no lane in the plan authored a migration or planned a deletion. | Fixed by adding `none`, and — because a member that changes no decision is decoration — by giving it a reader: a discharge rule refusing any effect declared `planned` that no requirement in the plan ever executes. Run against the plan as first authored, that rule refuses two of five effects and catches 26 of the false entries with no prose read at all. The corrected authoring is `planned` 3, `fake_only` 8, `none` 54. | An operator reading the distribution of a field on its first use and finding it implausible | Exhaustiveness over a closed vocabulary is only honest when the vocabulary can express every true answer, **including the empty one**. Force a choice from a set that cannot state the truth and authors will supply the nearest wrong value; the field then discriminates nothing while appearing rigorous, which is worse than the silence it replaced. Two tests before shipping such a set: can it express "this does not apply", and does each member change some decision? The second is what stops "not applicable" from being decoration — here it is the discharge rule, and without a reader the member should not have been added. |
| M13 | **A fix that existed in the template and never reached the runtime that ran.** M1's repair — carry the node's instruction to the reviewer and refuse rather than derive one — was implemented in the template's `code_review.py` and mirrored to the-library. The deployment at `lexgenius-pipeline/adws/` was never refreshed. The run this section's later rows repair therefore had every reviewer told, verbatim: "Make the gate '…' pass over selector '…', changing only the declared outputs." | The plan was not at fault: its projected `maestro-plan.v1` carries the correct 174-character instruction. The loss is entirely in the deployed reviewer, which has no `_node_goal` function at all. Against that brief, the M10 materializer *is* compliant, and the reviewers did the only work available — reading the diff for internal defects. `diff -rq` had been reporting `code_review.py` as differing between template and deployment the whole time; that difference was this. | Auditing what a reviewer had actually been handed, after its findings looked narrower than the defect | A lesson is extracted when it reaches the bytes that execute, not when it is committed. Three copies of a runtime held together by discipline will drift, and a `diff -rq` that reports a file as differing states a fact without stating its cost — nobody reads a filename as "every reviewer in this deployment is judging against a placeholder". Where copies must exist, hold them with a test that fails; where a deployment is reconciled by hand, name the behaviour each divergence changes, not the file. This is M1 recurring not in the code but in its distribution, which is the second time a §19 lesson has recurred after being extracted. |
| M14 | **A selector treated as one opaque string, in three places at once.** `plan_validate._gate_executable` decided §6.4's produced arm with `all(path in produced for path in paths)`, so a selector *mixing* paths the plan produces with paths that already exist matched neither arm's intent: it fell through to the base-time collection and was collected whole, at a base where the produced paths do not exist. The integration gate of `cmo-consolidation-l-r2` selects fourteen paths — thirteen written by its own lanes, one (`tests/unit/db/test_cmo_consolidation_models.py`) merged by an earlier plan at `86fbf54`. It was refused at ship: `GATE_EXECUTABLE`, "the integration gate's selector collects 0 case(s) at base, below the declared min_cases of 116". | One absent path zeroes the entire collection, and the runner refuses rather than degrading. Measured at base `77c608a8` with `.venv/bin/pytest` 9.1.1: the already-merged file alone collects **11**; the same file plus one not-yet-written path is `ERROR: file or directory not found` and collects **0**. The deeper error is the comparand — `min_cases` counts what the gate must pass *after* merge and was being applied to a base-time count — and the only moves the check left a plan author were to drop the merged test from the integration gate or to misdeclare `min_cases`, each of which weakens the evidence the gate exists to produce. Searching for the shape rather than waiting for it found two further instances, both latent. `scheduler.py` decided "is this selector a path this node produces?" against the **joined** selector string, which is true only of a single-path selector, so a multi-path node gate read ENVIRONMENTAL and retried an identically absent file until its budget was gone. `worktree.run_node_gate` appended the selector as **one** argv element, so a two-path selector reached the runner as the single path `tests/a.py tests/b.py`, which exists nowhere, and the gate exited 4 having collected nothing; it had worked by accident, because the caller's argv already carries the selector and the runner deduplicated the repeat. Neither sibling could fire on the plan in hand — all thirteen of its node gates name one path — so only the validator instance was observable. | A production plan refused at admission; its two siblings by searching the runtime for the same shape, before either could fire | A check whose only passing move is to weaken the evidence it exists to produce is a **defective check, not a strict one**. That is the tell, and it is readable before any diagnosis: when every option left to an author is "declare less", the predicate is wrong about its subject rather than demanding about it. Here it was wrong about two things at once and both generalize. A selector is a **set** of paths, so every predicate over one is quantified per path and must state which quantifier it means — `all`, `any`, or a partition. The three agree on a one-element set and on nothing else, which is precisely how a de-facto single-path convention keeps this class latent and then fires every instance of it at once when a plan finally spans two. And a threshold declared for one moment of a run is not a comparand at another: a check that must evaluate early needs its own weaker predicate — here, "the executable part of this selector names at least one case" — never a threshold borrowed from a later moment. |

### 19.2 What these rows have in common

Five of the first seven are the same failure at different layers: **a fact that was known at one point in the system was not stated there, and a downstream consumer supplied a plausible default instead of failing.** `instruction` became `""`; `pane_created` became `True`; an unreadable meter became "did not submit"; an undeclared outcome became "still running"; an unchecked route became "checked". In every case the default was chosen to be safe, and in every case the safe default was the failure. This is RC1 and RC5's shared root reached from a new direction: the defect is not that the default was wrong, it is that a default was reachable at all for a fact somebody upstream already held.

The sixth, M2, is the mirror image — a check that stated its universe *precisely*, in a comment, and stated the wrong one. Precision about the wrong authority is not a weaker defect than silence about the right one. M8 belongs with M2 rather than with the defaulting rows: nobody upstream held the fact of which interpreter should judge a lane, because the design never asked anyone to state it, and the runtime read it from the environment instead. An ambient authority is the limiting case of the wrong one.

**M9 through M12 name a second root, and forcing them into the first would lose what they teach.** In none of them was a known fact left unstated by an author who could have stated it. In each, the author knew the fact and **the vocabulary could not carry it**. The plan's schema had no field in which to say that a lane's behaviour lived in files the lane did not own (M9). It had no typed value in which to resolve a prohibition against a prescription, so both survived in prose and the builder had to guess (M10). Its enum named transports where the source document prohibited acts, so a lane using a transport innocently had no true cell to write (M11). Its dispositions could not express "this does not apply", so every author of an irrelevant effect wrote something false (M12). The first root is a **defaulting** failure: a fact existed and a default stood in for it. The second is an **expressiveness** failure: no default was reachable, because there was no field to default — the author wrote a value, believed it, and it was untrue because the vocabulary admitted no true one.

The two roots want opposite remedies, which is why the distinction is worth keeping. Against a defaulting failure, remove the default and fail closed. Against an expressiveness failure, failing closed makes it worse: exhaustiveness over an enum that cannot state the truth is precisely how M12 produced 48 false entries where silence would have produced none. The remedy there is to widen the vocabulary until every true answer is sayable — and then to check that each new member changes some decision, because a member nothing reads is decoration, and decoration in a typed field is more dangerous than prose, since it looks checked.

M14 is a third kind again, and it is the one this corpus had not yet named. Nothing was defaulted and nothing was inexpressible: the fact was stated, in a predicate, and the predicate quantified it wrongly. `all(path in produced for path in paths)` is a true statement about some selectors and a false one about the rest, and a de-facto convention — every selector anyone had written named one path — made the difference unobservable for as long as the convention held. Its two siblings were found by searching the runtime for the shape rather than by waiting for them, which is the first time in this corpus a defect class was closed ahead of its second instance firing.

M13 is neither root. Every fix it concerns was correct, stated, and committed; it simply never reached the bytes that executed. It is recorded here because its cost was indistinguishable from a design defect from inside the run — reviewers judging against a placeholder produce narrow findings, and narrow findings read as a weak reviewer rather than as an unpatched deployment.

Provenance across the fourteen has shifted, and the shift is the more useful finding. The first seven were found almost entirely by things breaking: four by a production run dying, one by an operator looking at a screen, two by tests only after the tests modelled the instrument faithfully. The later six were found by **reading artifacts a run had already produced** — the collector's dispatch, the reviewer's actual prompt bytes, the disposition census of a field on its first use, the deployment diff nobody had priced. M8 and M13 were caught before the next run paid for them. That is the first evidence in this corpus that a defect of this class can be found without spending a budget on it, and it suggests the cheap detector: after a run blocks, read what each actor was actually handed rather than what the design says it should have been. M14 adds a second detector beside it, and a cheaper one: once an instance of a defect has been characterised as a *shape*, search the runtime for that shape before fixing the instance. Two of M14's three instances had no run to be found by, and the search that found them cost one reading pass.

### 19.3 Incidents of 2026-08-19

| ID | Failure | Evidence | Found by | Lesson that binds future work |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| M15 | **A liveness clock that kept measuring an actor which had legitimately finished.** The worker quiesces the builder agent at `scheduler.py:1000` *before* it commits the work, runs the post gate, and dispatches the cross-vendor reviewer. From that moment the builder's session transcript cannot grow again, by construction. `Watchdog._check_attempt` went on measuring exactly that file — `watchdog.py:339` reads `attempt.extra["session_path"]` — and stalled the attempt when it had not grown for `execution.turn_timeout_s`. An attempt that had written a `success: true` envelope, been committed by the scheduler, had an adjudicated `results` row written, and was sitting legitimately in review was therefore killed and classified `ENVIRONMENTAL`. `PROCESS_DEAD` honours §9.7 through its injected `exit_status_observed` predicate; the turn clock beside it consulted nothing, and `NODE_TIMEOUT` above it consulted nothing either. | A threshold, not a race. Ten reviews in `run-9e9ac412669140039ae078601048f6c7`, dispatch-to-report latency 46s to 461s, and **exactly the two that exceeded `turn_timeout_s=300` were killed** — 461s and 368s — while the 260s review survived by 40 seconds. Latency tracks reviewer turn count at roughly 7s/turn across 15–64 turns, so it is unbounded model behaviour rather than a property of these two diffs; the 260s case did no worktree work at all, so worktree churn is not the driver. The configuration guarantees the outcome: `execution.turn_timeout_s: 300` against `reviewer.finalization_timeout_s: 600`, a reviewer permitted twice the budget of the clock that kills the attempt it belongs to. Both discarded verdicts were **rejections**, and both reviewers returned complete signed receipts 164s and 72s *after* the conviction, so the worker was never stuck. The accounting distortion is readable in the ledger: `attempts.extra_json.review_rejected` is present on all six correctly-settled rejections and absent on all three `ENVIRONMENTAL` kills, because a discarded rejection never reaches `_settle_review_rejection` and is never debited against `review_ceiling`. `lane-p1-canonical-object-key` merged only because the defect refunded it a review credit — had a1 settled correctly, a3 would have been its third counted rejection and the node would have blocked before a4 ever produced `c357cfa`, the commit now on `main`. | An operator watching a live run ask why a lane was burning attempts; the two latent siblings by searching the runtime for the shape before either could fire | **Ask which actor a clock is measuring, and whether that actor is still the one responsible.** A supervisor's signal is evidence about the actor it observes and about no other, and a phase boundary that hands the work to a different actor silently invalidates every clock still pointed at the first. The builder's transcript is a true statement about the builder and says nothing about a review; measuring it after the builder is deliberately dead is measuring the wrong subject, and the reading it produces — silence — is indistinguishable from the failure it was built to catch. §9.7's rule is the general remedy and it must be applied to **every** convicting signal rather than to the one that happened to be written first: an artifact a worker wrote outranks any status a supervisor observes about that worker. Two further consequences generalize. A guard that spares a completed attempt must not thereby unbound it — the correct move is to **defer** the last clock to a horizon already proven finite and strictly greater, not to exempt the attempt from it, and `SchedulerConfig.__post_init__` already refuses any configuration where `backstop_t_s` fails to exceed `greatest_run_window_s`, which is what makes that deferral safe rather than hopeful. And a misclassification is never confined to the attempt it mislabels: `ENVIRONMENTAL` and `SEMANTIC` debit different budgets, so classifying a rejection as an infrastructure failure silently refunds a review attempt and the ceiling stops meaning what it says. A run whose single success depended on that refund is not evidence the ceiling is sized correctly. |
| M16 | **A category error in a projection: the whole-suite gate was asked whether its selector was narrow.** `plan_finalization.review_objects` projected the plan's integration gate as `ObjectKind.GATE` — the same kind as a node's gate. That kind carries two node-scoped rubric questions: `gate.asserts_a_post_condition` ("the post-state of this node's own work — red before, green after") and the BLOCKING `gate.selector_is_scoped_to_this_node` ("does the selector name only what this node's outputs supply?"). Neither is a fact about the integration gate. §7.4 and §8.8 say the opposite of it: it is the one gate in this design that runs the whole suite, at the final head where every merged node's work is present at once, and §6.4's third executability arm and §19 M14 record that its selector legitimately spans tests an earlier plan already merged. The reviewer answered the question it was handed, correctly, and the plan FAILed on it. | The reviewer was not at fault and its message says so: "Integration selector includes inherited `tests/unit/db/test_cmo_consolidation_models.py` and `tests/unit/ingestion/test_cmo_canonical_object.py`; no current lane owns those outputs" — true, and exactly what §19 M14 blesses. `cmo-consolidation-l-r3`, digest `55a6ecb8`, 111 cells, 109 clear, one BLOCKING finding and the planted canary; `run start` refused `RUN_RECEIPT_NOT_PASS` at `maestro.py:2015`. The cost is set by two other rules meeting: receipts are create-once and replay is keyed on the digest alone (§6.5), so a FAIL is terminal for those bytes and no verb revokes it. A correct plan was therefore unrunnable forever, and the only route back was to re-author it as `cmo-consolidation-l-r4` — byte-different only in `plan_id` — costing a full gate, review, ship and finalize cycle to defeat a cache. Under the corrected projection r4 returned PASS: 110 cells, rubric `maestro-rubric.v2`, the integration gate carrying exactly `gate.selector_covers_the_merged_surface` and `gate.min_cases_is_meaningful`, both clear, canary firing. | An operator's run refused at start | **A kind in a review vocabulary is a claim about which questions are answerable of an object, not a label for what the object resembles.** A node gate and the integration gate are both gates and are opposite in scope, so one kind serving both guarantees that one of them is asked a question whose correct answer the architecture states in advance. The tell is the same one §19 M14 names and it was readable without any diagnosis: when a check's only passing move is to weaken the evidence the object exists to produce — here, to narrow the whole-suite gate until it stops being one — the predicate is wrong about its subject rather than demanding about it. Two consequences generalize. Severity is stamped from the rubric, so a category error lands as BLOCKING with no reviewer able to say otherwise: the design's refusal to let a reviewer emit a verdict (§6.5) is correct and puts the whole weight of correctness on the applicability matrix, which is code and is therefore testable — test it. And a terminal verdict needs an escape for the case where the machine, not the plan, produced it; B10 requires one and the implementation had only the half that forbids re-review. **Closed 2026-08-19** by `ReceiptStore.set_aside` and `maestro plan set-aside` (issue #38, §16.3 item 56, §17 item 132), which is why this row is the last plan a projection defect condemns rather than a standing cost. |
| M17 | **A bounded loop that could not converge, because any true finding rejected.** Six of `CODE_RUBRIC`'s seven checks are BLOCKING, and a cell with a `finding` on a BLOCKING check rejected the attempt. Acceptance therefore required an adversarial cross-vendor reviewer to find **zero** defects in a real diff — a predicate a competent reviewer fails forever, which is exactly what §3.6 A9 names. `review_ceiling` existed and was correct, and a bound over a non-convergent loop only decides how many attempts precede `BLOCKED`. A9 states its remedy as two alternatives, "bound the loop **or** accept graded findings"; Maestro had implemented one of them and read it as both. | The census, over all 27 review reports the production deployment had written: `diff.introduces_no_obvious_defect` carried a finding in **25**, `diff.implements_the_stated_instruction` in 13, `diff.gate_is_passed_on_the_merits` in 6. `lane-p2-s3-inventory` in `run-2a44d226e75a4be391a14f02b78a6d25` spent five attempts on five rejections, each round fixing the previous finding and earning a new one — swallowed `HeadObject` errors, truncated pagination, an unchecked zero-byte marker, a bucket-blind SHA grouping, a LIST/HEAD race, a `VersionId` AWS never supplies. **Every one of those findings was correct**, which is the whole of the row: the response to a true finding was wrong, not the finding of it. | Reading the review reports a blocked run had already written, after a lane exhausted its review ceiling with no two rejections alike | **A check's severity is a property of the question; how bad a true answer is, is a property of the instance, and one axis cannot carry both.** The fix is a second axis and not a weaker first one: the reviewer assigns a `grade` with a stated reason, code still stamps `severity` from the rubric and still derives the verdict, and a cell rejects only when its check is BLOCKING **and** its grade reaches the installation's threshold — so an ADVISORY check remains unable to reject however it is graded, and the reviewer still cannot express a verdict or a severity (§6.5). Three consequences generalize. Grading the bar is not lowering it *only if* the sub-threshold findings are recorded: every finding is written to a ledger beside the receipt, so a merged node's advisories are on disk under the same subject digest that admitted the merge. What decided a verdict belongs **inside the signed record** — the grade and the threshold it was measured against are both in the receipt from the first version that grades anything, because B8's rule is that a field added after receipts exist is optional forever, and a receipt carrying one without the other still records a conclusion nobody can re-derive from it. And a threshold is configuration, never plan content (§6.2): how tolerant an installation is of a true finding is a property of the installation, and a plan that could set it would be a plan that could grade its own review. |
| M18 | **One word naming two facts, and the operator's only stop control throwing away the run.** `resume_run` refused `ACCEPTED` and `CANCELLED` under one predicate. The two are not alike: `ACCEPTED` is terminal because the run reached its declared outcome, while `CANCELLED` is two outcomes wearing one word — a run the operator stopped with `run cancel`, where nothing was adjudicated and there is no result to protect, and a run given up on node by node through `abandon`, where every node was individually adjudicated as work the run should finish without. The store recorded **neither**, so the distinction could not be made at resume time, and that missing cause was the root defect rather than the refusal above it. The consequence: `run cancel` is the operator's only stop control, and using it discarded every merged, gate-verified, reviewed node in the run. | `run-75b96fd1f01e46989671771645ee6acc`, a twelve-lane plan: the same keystroke that stopped the machine threw away everything it had landed. The repair records the cause as a typed `CancelCause` at **both** levels — `runs.cancel_cause` written by `declare_outcome` in the transaction that declares it and rewritten by every later declaration, and `node_lifecycle.cancel_cause` stamped `RUN_CANCEL` by `cancel_run` and `ABANDONED` by `abandon`. The node-level column is not a convenience: a run whose operator abandoned one lane and then cancelled the run holds both causes at once, and a resume that reopened every `CANCELLED` node would resurrect the lane the operator gave up on. | An operator who stopped a run and found no way to restart it | **A stop request is not a verdict, and a state that cannot say which it was cannot be exited safely.** Where one state name covers a deliberate stop and an adjudicated decision, the fix is to record the cause as a typed field at the moment the state is written — not to loosen the guard, which would reopen the adjudicated case, and not to infer it afterwards from what else is in the row, which is the defaulting failure §19.2 names. The narrowness is the design: only from `CANCELLED`, only to `PENDING`, only for `RUN_CANCEL`, only by the operator. Two further things generalize. A `CANCELLED` with **no** recorded cause is refused rather than read as a pause — a migration invents no facts, and the guess that reads an unrecorded cancellation as a stop request is the guess that reopens an adjudicated run. And a fix that contradicts the architecture must **amend** it: this one made §7.3's "absolutely terminal" and §7.8's "no exit by design" false about `CANCELLED` on the day it landed, and a document left saying the opposite of the code is worse than one that never said it, because the next author reads it as a constraint. §7.3 and §7.8 now state three kinds of terminal. |
| M19 | **An escape gated on a node state that stood in for a run-level fact.** `retry`, `skip` and `abandon` each carried `require_state=(BLOCKED,)`, and `EscapeRefused` states what that gate was for: refusing "is what stops an escape from racing a scheduler that may still be alive". `BLOCKED` is not that fact. A scheduler that dies — killed, crashed, or its host restarted — leaves the node it was executing in `RUNNING` and declares nothing, which is M5's shape one level down. The node-state gate therefore excluded exactly the population it existed to serve: every escape verb the design offers refused the one state a dead scheduler produces, and a stranded node had no verb able to move it. | The gate now consults the fact it was standing in for. `RUNNING` is admitted only when `scheduler_liveness` returns `False`; `True` refuses `SCHEDULER_STILL_ALIVE` and `None` refuses `SCHEDULER_LIVENESS_UNKNOWN`, because unknown is not dead. `BLOCKED` is unchanged — a declaration already says the scheduler left. Admitting `RUNNING` is not sufficient on its own: the live attempt row is closed in the same transaction as the transition, or the next attempt collides with §10.3's partial unique index and §7.7 adjudicates a late arrival from the abandoned attempt as `ACCEPTED`. `abandon` already did this for `cancel_run`'s reason and now shares the same helper with `retry` and `skip`. The refusals are a typed `EscapeRefusal` vocabulary rather than one message, and the tests drive `maestro node retry` against a ledger reproducing the incident. | An operator with a stranded run trying every escape the design provides and being refused by all three, after the `run cancel` repair of M18 had landed | **A guard must test the fact it is protecting against, not a state that usually accompanies it.** The tell is readable without any diagnosis and it was written down: a guard whose own docstring names one fact while its predicate reads another is asserting a correlation, and a correlation fails first in the case the guard exists for — here, the scheduler that dies before it can declare, which is the entire population of stranded runs. Two consequences generalize. Where a guard's real subject is a run-level fact, read it at run level and give each refusal its own typed name, because "refused" alone cannot tell an operator whether the scheduler is still there or whether liveness could not be said, and those demand opposite next actions. And an escape that moves a node out of `RUNNING` must close that node's attempt row in the same transaction as the transition: the state and the row are one fact about one attempt, and a transition that updates half of it leaves the ledger able to accept work from an attempt the operator has already taken away. |
| M20 | **Machine identity read from a name a DHCP lease rewrites.** `scheduler_liveness` declines to answer when the recorded `scheduler_host` is not this machine, and that refusal is correct: a pid means nothing outside its own namespace, so a run recorded elsewhere must read `None` rather than being declared dead. The comparison was raw string equality over `socket.gethostname()`, which returns an FQDN whose domain suffix is assigned by whatever network the machine is attached to. The same laptop answered `Mac.attlocal.net` on one network and `Mac` on another. Every run recorded under the old suffix therefore read as belonging to a stranger, liveness returned `None` permanently, and — because M19's repair fails closed on unknown — every escape against those runs refused `SCHEDULER_LIVENESS_UNKNOWN`. The runs were unrecoverable on the only machine that could have recovered them. | `scheduler_host()` now stores the first DNS label and `_same_scheduler_host` compares short labels case-insensitively, so a ledger already holding an FQDN still matches its own first label after the suffix changes and no migration of stored rows is required. An empty identity matches nothing, so the ledger older than the column still reads unknown rather than same-host, and `Mac` against `OtherBox` is still a different machine. | M19's fix failing against the real ledger: the escape it had just made legal still refused, and the refusal named liveness rather than node state | **An identifier used as identity must be the part that cannot change for reasons unrelated to the thing it identifies.** A hostname is two facts joined by a dot — the machine's name and a network's current claim about where it sits — and only the first is identity; storing the pair and comparing it whole makes a network change look like a different computer. Ask of any stored identifier which of its components a third party can rewrite, and compare only what is left. The second lesson is about what a correct fail-closed rule costs: M19 refuses on unknown and is right to, and that rightness converts every source of a spurious "cannot tell" into a permanent refusal rather than a retry. A fail-closed guard makes the precision of its own input load-bearing, so shipping one obliges an audit of every path that can manufacture its unknown. |
| M21 | **A version field naming one thing and used to discriminate another.** `rubric_version` names the rubric — which checks apply to which object kinds — and receipts persist it because an applicability matrix is only interpretable against the rubric that produced it (M16). M17's grading repair added two keys to the receipt payload, `Receipt.reject_at` and a per-cell `grade`, and correctly left the label at `maestro-rubric.v2`, since no check's question had changed. But `Receipt.from_bytes` was using that label as its *schema* discriminator: `maestro-rubric.v1` meant no `reject_at`, anything else meant both new keys were required. `maestro-rubric.v2` therefore named two incompatible payload schemas at once, and every signed v2 receipt written before the grading fields existed became unparseable — bytes untouched, signature still verifying, refused by the parser as failing the frozen schema. | The 110-cell PASS receipt for `cmo-consolidation-l-r4` recorded in M16 is exactly such a receipt, and `run start` against its digest could no longer read it. The first repair had widened the same mistake rather than fixing it: it made `reject_at` required *per rubric version*, which restored `maestro-rubric.v1` and left pre-grading v2 broken, because it kept the label as the discriminator. `from_bytes` now discriminates on the payload — `reject_at` present means the graded shape and every cell must carry a `grade`; absent means the pre-grading shape and both read honestly `None` rather than defaulted. The shapes cannot be mixed: a payload carrying `reject_at` with an ungraded cell is refused, as is a graded cell in a payload without it, so nothing written today can omit either field. | Replaying the live receipt store after the fix that restored v1 replay, and finding the receipt of the very run this section's M16 row records still refused | **A version field is a promise about exactly one thing, and using it to discriminate a second is a defect from the day the two stop moving together.** The rubric version answers "which checks applied"; the payload schema answers "which keys are present". They coincided until one change moved one and not the other, and that change was *right* to leave the rubric label alone — which is what makes this class dangerous, because the defect is introduced by a correct decision elsewhere. Two remedies generalize. Discriminate on the evidence itself, the presence of the key whose presence is in question, rather than on a label merely correlated with it: a payload is the authority on its own shape. And where a shape must genuinely be versioned, version the shape and nothing adjacent to it, or every future field addition re-runs this incident. §3.6 B8 sets the price: a field added after receipts exist is optional forever, so the repair can never be to require the key retroactively — the parser must read both shapes for as long as any receipt of the older one survives, and every such split is permanent. |
| M22 | **A rubric question that overran the contract the same prompt declared, producing a demand no diff could satisfy.** `CODE_RUBRIC`'s `diff.implements_the_stated_instruction` asked "does this diff do what the node was asked to do" with nothing bounding the demand to the paths the node was permitted to write, and `diff.gate_is_passed_on_the_merits` asked about the whole of the gate the same way. `ReviewHandoff.render` **did** show the reviewer those paths — under "## Paths this node was permitted to write" — and a reviewer that read them and ignored them for the question was reading the rubric correctly. Where the instruction named work reachable only from another node's declared output, the two rules then closed on each other: `plan_validate`'s single-producer check forbade this node from declaring that path, `worktree`'s permission check convicted any attempt that wrote it, and the reviewer rejected every attempt that did not. No diff satisfied both, and the loop ran to the review ceiling. | `run-2a44d226e75a4be391a14f02b78a6d25`, node `lane-p4-enrichment-ordering`: BLOCKED `REVIEW_BUDGET_EXHAUSTED` after eight attempts, six of them review rejections on `diff.implements_the_stated_instruction` at grade `error` with a distinct `review_subject_digest` each time. The findings read "changes no production caller to use them", "no existing production entry point is changed to import it", "the diff adds no import from an existing production module". The work was not the problem: the gate's case count rose 13 → 25 → 27 → 28 → 32 → 34, green on every attempt, with `results.adjudication=ACCEPTED` each time. | An operator reading the lifecycle store after a lane blocked on a budget, and finding the reviewer had asked six times for a file the plan forbade the node to touch | **A declared contract bounds every question asked beside it, or it is decoration.** B9 says the reviewer's input is a declared contract of goal, `produces` and acceptance. Presenting that contract and then asking a question whose truthful answer can require an edit outside it is the violation — the reviewer is not wrong, the rubric is. Three things generalize. **Grading cannot carry this, and reaching for it is the tempting wrong repair**: the honest grade for undelivered work is ERROR by the scale's own anchor, so telling a reviewer to soften it is telling it to misreport a consequence, and A9's remedy is grading the bar rather than lowering it. The missing axis is not *how bad* but *where the fix would land*, and it is a separate enum (`FindingScope`) answered per finding, required from the schema's first version because B8's rule is that a field added later is optional forever. **The audit is per shape, not per instance**: a question is unbounded exactly when a truthful answer can demand a forbidden path, which is two of `CODE_RUBRIC`'s seven — the other five are answered by writing *less* (`no_unrelated_change_rides_along`, both `file.*` checks), are ADVISORY and cannot reject (`is_coherent_with_its_surroundings`), or already bound themselves to the changed code (`introduces_no_obvious_defect`). **And a rubric edit is a receipt-compatibility event**: `review_digest` binds `rubric_version`, so changing what a question asks without moving the version replays an answer given to a different question — hence `maestro-code-rubric.v2`. |
| M23 | **A ledger of standing constraints with one slot per surface, held only in the process that observed them.** `GuidanceLedger` existed because verification and review had been overwriting a single last-failure string, and it fixed that by giving each acceptance surface its own slot — which left the same defect intact *within* a surface. `with_verification` and `with_review` **replaced** their slot, so a node that failed the permission check twice carried only the second offending path into its next prompt, and a node the reviewer rejected twice carried only the second set of findings. The justification written into the docstring was that newer evidence supersedes older, "which is how a fixed finding retires" — and it does not hold, because each surface re-evaluates a **different diff** every attempt: a path absent from the latest evaluation may be absent because the attempt stopped writing that file, not because the constraint was satisfied, and the surface's output cannot distinguish the two. Beneath that sat the larger half: `Scheduler._guidance` was a plain dict built by the running process and rebuilt from nothing in `project()`, while every other piece of state that survives a scheduler is derived from the store. A resumed run therefore dispatched its builder with **no guidance at all**, having already spent the attempts that produced it. | The two halves compound in the direction that costs most. `_settle_failure`'s semantic arm writes the ledger entry and then, if the semantic ceiling is reached, takes `mark_blocked` rather than `fail_attempt` — so the attempt whose guidance is most likely to be wanted is the one belonging to a node an operator is about to grant extra attempts and resume, and it was the entry guaranteed to be discarded. Both surfaces already had a durable row to ride: `attempts.extra_json`, which `REVIEW_REJECTED_KEY` has used for the review budget since that budget existed, and which `mark_blocked` and `fail_attempt` both accept an `attempt_extra` for. Nothing needed to be recorded that was not already being recorded next to it. | Reading `retry_policy.py` beside the incident it cites — `run-32b19abadf4a4d6b801ae0f4456976c7`, blocked after five attempts — and finding that the fix for that incident had left the same shape one level in | **A ledger that accumulates is one whose entries are appended and whose reader is bounded — never one whose slot is overwritten to keep it small.** Replacement is truncation with no marker: it discards evidence silently, at the moment of writing, with no record that anything was dropped and no way for a reader to know the history was ever longer. Bounding belongs at the **rendering** boundary where the limit actually exists, because only there is it known what the budget is and which end of the history is expendable. That placement has a corollary this repair had to pay: once history accumulates, the truncation's **direction** becomes a decision. `_fit` drops trailing lines, and history renders oldest-first, so the obvious composition would have kept the finding an attempt had already addressed and dropped the one it still had to fix — a bound that is correct about size and inverted about content. Drop whole entries from the oldest end, and say so in the text. The second lesson is §19.2's own root reached from the storage side, and it recurs here for the third time: **derived state that only a live process writes is unwritten in exactly the case it is needed.** M5 named it for `latest_outcome` and the remedy was to derive the run's state from durable node rows. This is the same shape for retry guidance, and the tell is mechanical rather than diagnostic — ask of any in-memory accumulator whether `project()` rebuilds it, and if the answer is no, it is not state, it is a cache of the current process's luck. |
| M24 | **A retry that landed on the actor which had succeeded, because the failure was recorded against the attempt rather than against the reviewer.** `Scheduler._attempt_body` dispatches the cross-vendor reviewer after the builder's work is committed, its post-node gate is green, its permission check has passed and its post-commit cleanliness check has passed. The `except cr.ReviewStalled` arm classified that failure ENVIRONMENTAL — correct, since a reviewer that never reported says nothing about the code — and then settled it with `_settle_failure`, which releases the **attempt**. The node returned to `PENDING`, the next attempt built a new worktree at the integration head, and the builder ran again from scratch; the committed tree was left on its own `refs/heads/maestro/{run}/{node}/a{n}` ref with nothing pointing at it. The classification named the right class and the wrong subject: ENVIRONMENTAL is a statement about the machine, and the machine that failed was the reviewer's, not the builder's. | `lane-p5-gap-policy` in `run-2a44d226e75a4be391a14f02b78a6d25`, four attempts: a1 250 turns committed `df98283` with 10 gate cases passing, a4 164 turns committed `c362efd` with 12, a5 163 turns with 16 and no commit, a6 220 turns committed `46fde88` with 19. Four accepted envelopes, four passing gates, **zero verdicts**, and the node blocked `ENVIRONMENTAL_BUDGET_EXHAUSTED` having never once been reviewed — roughly 800 builder turns where at most 250 were needed. `df98283` was checked by hand afterwards against the plan's own verifier and was sound. The gate counts are the second cost and the less obvious one: each restart replayed the retry guidance ledger, so every cycle produced a *larger* implementation of the same requirement, grown by failures that were never the builder's. And this is not compensation for an unreliable reviewer — over the same corpus the reviewer completed 33 reviews, verdicts in both directions, against 7 that took their receipt lock and never wrote one, of which two never launched at all and four were killed by Maestro itself in 38.5–128.9 seconds. | An operator reading a blocked node's attempt rows and finding four builder successes and no review | **A retry has a subject as well as a class, and naming the class correctly does not make the subject right.** ENVIRONMENTAL was always the right *class* for a reviewer stall; what was missing is that the failure belonged to a different actor than the unit being discarded. Ask of any retry which actor failed, then which artifact the retry destroys, and refuse the pairing where a succeeding actor's durable output is thrown away for another's fault. Three things generalize. **The durable output is what makes the narrower retry possible**, and it already existed: `commit_measured_delta` advances the attempt's ref by compare-and-swap, so the builder's work outlives the process the moment it commits, and the re-dispatch needs only to read that ref *back* and refuse when it no longer holds the committed SHA — a proven subject, never a remembered one. **A budget can be respent without being widened**: the re-dispatch spends the same ENVIRONMENTAL allowance the discard used to spend, on reviewers rather than on builders, so nothing about the bound changed and the exhausted case still settles at the reason it always wrote. **And a narrower retry must be checked against the clocks it now runs inside** — re-dispatching without regard for the attempt's own §7.6 window would convert a reviewer stall into a `NODE_TIMEOUT` and discard the same commit one horizon later, which is the failure the repair exists to stop, so the decision reads the window's remaining headroom against the stall's own elapsed time and settles rather than starting a review it cannot finish. A fourth thing was found while repairing this and deserves its own sentence: `ReviewStalled.signal` is a typed `FinalizationSignal` that had **zero readers and zero durable representations** — both window factories recorded stalls with `lambda _s, _sig, _e: None`, and the settle arm wrote one reason string for all four signals — so nothing on disk told ACTOR_ABANDONED from PROCESS_DEAD, TURN_TIMEOUT or WINDOW_TIMEOUT. §3.6 B15 makes that a build failure; the re-dispatch row is now its reader and its record. |
| M25 | **A destructive verb that proved *whose* state it removed and never *whether that state was in use*.** `deliver release-run` frees the integration branch a previous run's worktree holds, delegating to `_reclaim_stranded_integration_worktree`, which ran `git worktree remove --force`. That function enforced one boundary, correctly and at length: only a worktree inside this repository's own run root is ever removed, never an operator's checkout of the same branch. It enforced nothing about *when*. Neither it nor its caller read `latest_outcome` or any lifecycle state, so a run's integration checkout was removed on identical terms whether the run had been accepted, was blocked awaiting an operator, or was live with a scheduler attached. `--force` discarded uncommitted content in the tree silently on every one of those paths. The same gap sat on `_deliver_remove_plan`, which bounded its `shutil.rmtree` to `plans_dir` and never asked whether a resumable run was keyed to the plan bytes it was deleting — and runs are found by plan digest (`_deliver_accepted_run`), so clearing the directory destroys the evidence the run is resumed by, not merely a plan about to be rewritten. | `run-2a44d226e75a4be391a14f02b78a6d25` sat `BLOCKED` for hours with seven of twelve nodes `MERGED` and its integration checkout holding those merges; `deliver release-run` against that plan would have force-removed it. Recovery is not free even where nothing is lost: `scheduler.py` re-proves every `MERGED` node's `output_sha` against the integration head on resume and raises `DurableOutputIdentityError` when it is not an ancestor, so a reconstructed checkout fails the run later rather than at the moment it was destroyed. The verb's own docstring already named the intent — it is for "the backlog: worktrees stranded by runs that predate that release" — and a blocked-but-resumable run is not backlog. The docstring named it; nothing held the code to it. The acceptance existed too: `DeliverReleaseSafetyTest` had been written in a deployment and failed there, three cases against an unimplemented predicate. | Reading the verb's boundary while discharging #71, before any operator ran it against the blocked run | Two predicates govern every destructive verb, and a verb that has only one is not "narrow", it is unfinished. *Whose* state is this — answered by containment against a root the system configured. *Is anything still using it* — answered by the owner's own recorded lifecycle state, and by nothing else (§1.2). The first is easy to write and easy to document at length, which is exactly why its presence reads as diligence and masks the absence of the second. Three rules follow. Derive "still in use" by **inverting the predicate that already decides resumability** — `lifecycle.resume_run`'s — rather than authoring a second definition of "over for good"; two definitions is how a verb starts deleting checkouts an operator has every right to resume. Answer every unreadable case as *in use*: a missing ledger, an absent row, an older schema, a database that will not open — none is evidence a run finished, and fail-closed here costs a refusal while fail-open costs merged work. And **never force**. `worktree.remove_attempt_worktree` is the shape to imitate: it refuses on unproven ancestry and uses unforced `worktree remove` so git's own refusal becomes evidence about the checkout rather than noise to override. `--force` on a tree that should be empty converts "this is not what you think it is" into silence. The operator escape is a flag they type, never a default (§11.3). |
| M26 | **A projection that narrowed a field rather than dropping it, and was therefore invisible to every consumer.** `plan_contract_ingress.project_draft` mapped `lanes[].title` onto `nodes[].instruction` — the builder's whole brief and the reviewer's declared goal — and dropped `requirements[].text`, the sentence that bounds the lane's scope. `maestro-plan.v1` carries no `requirement_id`, so nothing downstream could recover the text by reference either, and `prompt_assets` — the only other node-level channel — was empty on every node and has no reader on the running side at all. This is §3.6 B9 one projection upstream of M1: M1 left the field blank, this one left it plausible. | Measured over the four executable plans in the `lexgenius-pipeline` deployment, 51 agent nodes: `nodes[].instruction == lanes[].title` **verbatim for 12 of 12** nodes in the plan under test, **2,256 bytes of titles standing in for 18,824 bytes** of `requirements[].text`, and `prompt_assets` empty on all 51. `lane-p4-enrichment-ordering` carried 199 bytes of a 971-byte requirement. In `run-2a44d226e75a4be391a14f02b78a6d25` that node **burned its entire review ceiling — six reviews, every one rejecting on `diff.implements_the_stated_instruction`** with findings of the form "changes no production caller to use them". The dropped remainder of its requirement names the four production callers and says wiring them "is explicitly out of scope for this lane and for this plan, which declares none of those files as an output; a later plan owns that migration". The plan author had already asked and answered the reviewer's question; admission passed and `plan validate` had nothing to refuse, because the declared outputs and surface were a truthful record of that exclusion. The `out_of_scope` grade added to `maestro-code-rubric.v2` does not close it: it lets a reviewer record an unreachable instruction without rejecting on it, but a reviewer that cannot read the exclusion cannot grade against it. | Reading the four plans on disk while discharging #54, after the run had already blocked — §19's third provenance, costing no budget of its own | **A populated field cannot be audited by its consumers.** M1's dropped `instruction` was findable precisely because every reviewer received `""`; this one was unfindable because every reviewer received something plausible and relayed it faithfully. Where a projection *narrows* a value instead of dropping it, no downstream check can separate a summary from the contract, and totality guards of the `_assert_projection_is_total` kind cannot help — the field is carried. The only place the loss is visible is the projection itself, which is where the test has to sit, and it has to assert on content the summary would not contain rather than on the field's presence. Two corollaries. First, when a field is widened because what it carried was too small, ask what the **authored unit** is rather than reaching for the next-larger field to hand: this one went lane-id → lane-title → requirement, and the first widening was the wrong question answered correctly, which bought a year of looking fixed. Second, **a channel with no writers is B15's field with no readers, and the repair is never to add the writer** — routing the text through `prompt_assets` would have meant building both ends of a channel that nothing reads, while `instruction` already had both. Carry the value on the channel that is already read, and size-check the widened result at the chokepoint that already exists (M6) rather than beside the change. |
| M27 | **Every actor Maestro launched could hand its work to a second model, and the receipt it then signed named only itself.** `launcher.build_omp_argv` and `build_claude_argv` passed no capability constraint of any kind, so the tool set an actor held was whatever its omp `--pm-profile` or the Claude install happened to grant — including omp's `task` (spawn a sub-agent) and `hub` (start and await named background jobs), both members of that binary's `BUILTIN_TOOL_NAMES`. A reviewer that spawned a sub-task, blocked on it, and relayed its conclusion produced a verdict originating somewhere its signature does not name, which is §3.6 B12 and §1.2 at once. Nothing downstream could tell: the transcript, the receipt and the Ed25519 signature all name the actor Maestro launched. The capability surface existed and had been **dropped at the cutover** rather than never built — the SSSF sibling path still carries it (`AgentConfig.tools` → `agent_pi.py:258` `--tools`, `agent_cc.py:174` `--tools`/`--allowedTools`, documented at §2.7), so this is RC6's shape: the model moved and the accumulated semantics did not. | `run-2a44d226e75a4be391a14f02b78a6d25`, re-derived from the session transcripts rather than from the receipt count: **41** review digests, 39 of which launched, **8** lock-only (two of those never launched at all). **23 of the 39 launched reviews spawned a named sub-task and blocked on it. 17 of those wrote a signed receipt**, every one attesting `{"model": "openai-codex/gpt-5.6-luna", "route": "omp"}` over a verdict produced by a `gpt-5.6-terra:high` or `:xhigh` sub-task named for the review itself — `PdfValidationReview`, `EnrichmentReviewer`, `ManifestReviewer`, `DedupDiffReviewer` — **three of them PASS**. The remaining 6 were SIGHUPed still holding `hub op=wait`, wrote no receipt, and were absorbed as environmental stalls that cancelled and rebuilt the builder (M24, #90). The two populations are one behaviour interrupted at different moments, not two defects: the pending call at exit separates them completely, and the delegation does not. Why the builder never did it is configuration nobody versioned — the reviewer's `openai-performance` profile sets `task.agentModelOverrides.task: openai-codex/gpt-5.6-terra:auto` in `~/.omp/profiles/…/agent/config.yml`, the builder's `grok` profile points the same key at its own model — so the fact deciding whether a receipt tells the truth lived in an unsigned per-machine file Maestro passed by name and never read. | Re-deriving #94's discriminator from the transcripts to check a count, and finding its claim that no receipt-producing review had delegated false in 17 cases | **Constrain what an actor may *invoke* at dispatch, not only what it may *write* after the fact — a delegated judgement leaves no diff to convict.** `permissions.py` had one axis, write authority, enforced by comparing the repository against itself afterwards, and that axis is correct and sufficient for writes precisely because a write is durable and undoable. Delegation is neither: by the time anything could look, the only surviving artifact is a signed receipt saying the right actor produced it, so the remedy has to be removal of the capability before the process starts.
